It does. \quad \textbf{(1)} \emph{the four rectangular subregions and the one pixel overlap, credited inline} $\leftarrow$ [Kyprianidis 2011, \S2:] ``Another popular edge-preserving smoothing filter is the Kuwahara filter \textbf{[Kuwahara et al.\ 1976]}. The general idea behind this filter is to \textbf{divide the filter kernel into four rectangular subregions that overlap by one pixel}. The filter response is then defined by the \textbf{mean of a subregion with minimum variance}.'' The credit marker sits on the sentence \emph{immediately preceding} the structural description, so the 1976 paper is what the structure is being attributed to. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the same attribution, independently, in the other paper} $\leftarrow$ [Kyprianidis et al.\ 2009, \S2:] ``Classical filters in this field are the \textbf{well-known Kuwahara filter [KHEK76]} and its variants [TT77, NM79]\ldots \textbf{The Kuwahara and Tomita-Tsuji filter use rectangular subregions}, while the Nagao-Matsuyama filter uses a rotated elongated bar mask.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that the fixed rectangular arrangement is what makes it isotropic} $\leftarrow$ [same paper, Abstract and \S3:] the contribution is a generalisation ``\textbf{adapted to the local shape of features}'', which is what the classical form lacks.}
  {Primary not held. Verified against \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_multicale\_209.pdf} \S2 and \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_kuwahara.pdf} \S2. \textbf{Status, stated plainly: this is the strongest verification available for this key, and it is not a primary-source confirmation.} \S9 normally bars marking an entry confirmed without the primary. That rule exists to stop attribution resting on a paraphrase. Here it does not: Kyprianidis attaches the inline credit to the precise structural claim the thesis makes, in two separate papers, so the thesis is citing the work the claim originates from rather than the work it was read in. Nidanur confirmed on 2026-08-25 that the primary cannot be obtained, and directed that the origin credit be verified this way and kept. \textbf{Bibliographic fields corroborated across both reference lists.} [2011:] ``Kuwahara, M., Hachimura, K., Ehiu, S., and Kinoshita, M. 1976. \emph{Processing of ri-angiocardiographic images}. In Digital processing of biomedical images, K. Preston and M. Onoe, Eds. Plenum Press, 187--203.'' [2009:] ``[KHEK76] Kuwahara M., Hachimura K., \textbf{Eiho} S., Kinoshita M.: Digital processing of biomedical images. Plenum Press, 1976, pp.~187--203.'' Between them these supply the chapter title, the editors, the publisher, the year and the page range, and the two agree on all of it. \textbf{One discrepancy, resolved.} The third author is spelled \emph{Ehiu} in the 2011 reference list and \emph{Eiho} in the 2009 one. \texttt{references.bib} follows \textbf{Eiho}, which is the correct form: it is an attested Japanese surname and it is what the 2009 list, the DOI record and every other citation of this chapter give. The 2011 spelling is a typographical error in that paper's bibliography. \textbf{Duplicate PDFs removed from the Articles folder the same day} (\texttt{anisotropic\_kuwahara copy.pdf} and \texttt{anisotropic\_multicale\_209 copy.pdf}), both byte-identical to the files kept.}

\citentry*
  {kyprianidis2011 --- Kyprianidis (2011)}
  {Ch.2 Background $\rightarrow$ Edge-Preserving Filtering}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Any subregion straddling a boundary carries a higher variance and loses this comparison. Interior regions therefore flatten towards uniform fills while boundaries stay sharp and the result resembles painterly brushwork. The classical formulation sets $N = 4$, and its isotropic rectangular subregions overlap their neighbours by a single pixel. \textbf{They sit in fixed positions, meaning that no orientation is favoured over another.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the classical filter's construction} $\leftarrow$ [\S2 Related Work, printed p.~56:] ``Another popular edge-preserving smoothing filter is the Kuwahara filter [Kuwahara et al.\ 1976]. The general idea behind this filter is to \textbf{divide the filter kernel into four rectangular subregions that overlap by one pixel}. The filter response is then defined by the mean of a subregion with minimum variance. The Kuwahara filter \textbf{produces clearly noticeable artifacts, which are due to the use of rectangular subregions}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the orientation contrast the thesis draws} $\leftarrow$ [Kyprianidis et al.\ 2009, \S2:] ``The \textbf{Kuwahara and Tomita-Tsuji filter use rectangular subregions}, while the Nagao-Matsuyama filter uses a \textbf{rotated elongated bar mask}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_multicale\_209.pdf}, \S2, with the orientation contrast from the 2009 paper, \S2. \textbf{REPLACED 2026-08-25.} The entry previously held the 2011 paper's \emph{abstract}, describing Kyprianidis's own multi-scale contribution: ``A multi-scale computation scheme is proposed that simultaneously propagates local orientation estimates and filtering results up a low-pass filtered pyramid.'' The thesis sentence is about the \textbf{classical 1976 filter}, not the 2011 method. The two shared only the word \emph{orientation}. The correct passage sits on p.~56 of the same PDF and is already quoted by the neighbouring \texttt{kuwahara1976} entry. \textbf{Inference note:} ``No orientation is favoured over another'' is not stated literally in either paper. It follows from the fixed rectangular geometry, and quote~(2) is the closest explicit support, since it contrasts those rectangles against a filter that does rotate its mask.}

\citentry*
  {kyprianidis2009 --- Kyprianidis et al. (2009)}
  {Ch.2 Background $\rightarrow$ Edge-Preserving Filtering}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Nevertheless, Kyprianidis et al. recovered that orientation in an anisotropic formulation that aligns the sampling window with local image structure, estimated through the structure tensor. \textbf{This tensor is calculated directly from the RGB values of the input rather than from a luminance channel.} \emph{[For an image $\mathbf{f}$ with colour-valued partial derivatives $\mathbf{f}_x$ and $\mathbf{f}_y$, the smoothed tensor is the $2\times2$ matrix --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``recovered that orientation in an anisotropic formulation\ldots estimated through the structure tensor''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``It is based on a generalization of the Kuwahara filter that is \textbf{adapted to the local shape of features, derived from the smoothed structure tensor}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``calculated directly from the RGB values of the input''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.1 Orientation and Anisotropy Estimation:] ``The local orientation and anisotropy estimation is based on the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the structure tensor [BWBM06]. \textbf{We calculate the structure tensor directly from the RGB values of the input} [KD08].''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_kuwahara.pdf}, Abstract and \S3.1. \textbf{Sentence added 2026-08-25, spotted by Nidanur in the primary.} The thesis implied the colour computation through ``colour-valued partial derivatives'' and ``each dot product runs over those channels'', but never stated it. The contrast is worth stating because \S2.4 has already established that gradient operators run on a \emph{luminance} channel, so computing the tensor on RGB instead is a departure rather than a detail. Placement chosen by Nidanur: it follows the structure-tensor sentence and picks it up with ``This tensor'', rather than sitting after the equation. The post-equation clause was changed from ``runs over the colour channels'' to ``runs over those channels'' so that it refers back instead of restating the same fact. \textbf{PROVENANCE CAVEAT, RAISED AND LEFT AS IS. Not a defect, but on the record (\S5.3a).} The \S3.1 passage carries two inline credits, and both point away from this key. The structure tensor itself is credited to \textbf{[BWBM06]}, Brox, Weickert, Burgeth and Mrazek (2006), \emph{Non-linear structure tensors}, Image and Vision Computing 24(1):41--55. The RGB-direct computation is credited to \textbf{[KD08]}, Kyprianidis and D\"ollner (2008), \emph{Image Abstraction by Structure Adaptive Filtering}, Proc.\ EG UK TPCG, pp.~51--58. Neither is held, and the first also bears on the structure tensor equation itself, since the tensor is Brox et al.'s construct. \texttt{kyprianidis2009} is nonetheless the right key here, because the thesis sentences describe \emph{what the anisotropic Kuwahara filter does}, and the 2009 paper is authoritative for its own construction. The thesis does not claim to introduce either technique. If the origin credits are ever wanted, both PDFs must be obtained first.}

\citentry*
  {kyprianidis2009 --- Kyprianidis et al. (2009)}
  {Ch.2 Background $\rightarrow$ Edge-Preserving Filtering}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} where each dot product runs over those channels and the Gaussian $G_\rho$ smooths every component so that the estimate reflects a neighbourhood rather than one pixel. Its eigenvectors give the dominant local orientation and the eigenvalues indicate how strong that orientation is. An elliptical kernel derived from the two aligns the brush marks with salient contours. \textbf{The ellipse is partitioned into $N$ sectors in place of the rectangles, and typical values are $N = 4$ or $N = 8$.} \emph{[That smoothing step is itself a convolution across the image, so it requires a render pass of its own. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``The ellipse is partitioned into $N$ sectors in place of the rectangles''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.2:] ``We \textbf{divide the disc into $N$ equal sectors} by defining characteristic functions which are 1 over the sector and 0 otherwise.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``typical values are $N = 4$ or $N = 8$''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4 Results:] ``\ldots with our implementation using $\sigma_r = 3.0$, $\sigma_s = 1.0$ and \textbf{$N = 8$}. A possibility to increase the processing speed is to \textbf{reduce the number of sectors to $N = 4$}. This will result in slightly less sharp boundaries between color regions.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_kuwahara.pdf}, \S3.2 and \S4. \textbf{New citation added 2026-08-25.} The sentence is new and closes the $N$ thread opened in the preceding paragraph: the reader is told why the count is carried as a symbol rather than fixed at the classical~4. \textbf{Origin rule applied (\S5.3a).} Kyprianidis 2011 states the same pair of values in a tidier form, ``typical values $N = 4$ or $N = 8$'', but it is restating the 2009 paper's own implementation note quoted above. The citation therefore goes to 2009, where the values originate, not to the later paper the phrasing was read in.}

\section*{Chapter: Related Work}

\citentry*
  {mori1970 --- Mori (1970)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} He proposed that human comfort towards an artificial figure increases with its human likeness. This rise holds only up to a critical threshold. Beyond it, subtle imperfections cause a sharp drop in comfort. The drop persists until the figure appears almost fully human. \textbf{Only then is comfort recovered}~\textbf{[cite]}.}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``comfort\ldots increases with its human likeness''} $\leftarrow$ ``\textbf{as robots appear more humanlike, our sense of their familiarity increases} until we come to a valley.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``This rise holds only up to a critical threshold.''} $\leftarrow$ ``\ldots increases \textbf{until we come to a valley. I call this relation the ``uncanny valley.''}'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``subtle imperfections cause a sharp drop in comfort''} $\leftarrow$ ``this kind of prosthetic hand is \textbf{too real and when we notice it is prosthetic, we have a sense of strangeness}\ldots there is \textbf{no longer a sense of familiarity. It is uncanny}\ldots so the prosthetic hand is at the bottom of the valley.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``The drop persists until the figure appears almost fully human. Only then is comfort recovered.''} $\leftarrow$ \S``The significance of the uncanny'': ``\textbf{In Figure 1, a healthy person is at the top of the second peak.}''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/uncanny1970.pdf} (MacDorman \& Minato translation, \S\S``Valley of familiarity and ``The significance of the uncanny). \textbf{Each numbered pair maps one thesis sentence to the passage that supports it.} The citation sits on the last sentence of the block and covers all four.}

\citentry*
  {mori1970 --- Mori (1970)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Figure~3.1 caption]} The vertical axis represents comfort level (\emph{shinwakan}). The horizontal axis represents degree of human likeness. \textbf{The solid curve describes affective response to still figures, while the dashed curve describes figures in motion}~\textbf{[cite]}.}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``vertical axis\ldots comfort level (shinwakan)''} $\leftarrow$ Figure~1 y-axis is labelled \textbf{``comfort level (\emph{shinwakan})''} in the reproduced figure; the prose renders the same construct as ``familiarity''. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``horizontal axis\ldots degree of human likeness''} $\leftarrow$ Figure~1 x-axis is labelled \textbf{``human likeness''}, marked at \textbf{50\%} and \textbf{100\%}. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``solid curve\ldots still figures\ldots dashed curve\ldots figures in motion''} $\leftarrow$ Figure~1 key, upper left: \textbf{``moving'' (dashed line) / ``still'' (solid line)}; body text \S``The effects of movement'': ``\textbf{As shown in Figure 1, adding movement changes the shape of the uncanny valley by exaggerating the peaks and valley.}''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/uncanny1970.pdf}, Figure~1 and \S``The effects of movement. \textbf{Translation note:} the later MacDorman \& Kageki (2012) translation splits this into two figures (Fig.~1 still, Fig.~2 moving). The thesis reproduces the older single-figure version, where both curves share one plot, so the caption is correct for the figure actually shown.}

\citentry*
  {mori1970 --- Mori (1970)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Mori plotted separate curves for still and moving figures. Both follow the same general shape, but the curve for moving figures drops far deeper into negative comfort. Moreover, the reference points that Mori placed inside the valley, such as a corpse, a zombie and a prosthetic hand, share a common characteristic. Each closely resembles a human at first glance. \textbf{The eerie response arises only once the discrepancy registers}~\textbf{[cite]}. \emph{[Hence the place where that drop occurs is the most problematic region of the curve. --- thesis inference, uncited.]}}
  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``separate curves for still and moving figures''} $\leftarrow$ Figure~1 key: \textbf{``moving'' (dashed) / ``still'' (solid)}. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``the curve for moving figures drops far deeper''} $\leftarrow$ ``adding movement changes the shape of the uncanny valley by \textbf{exaggerating the peaks and valley}''; ``if we \textbf{add movement to a prosthetic hand, which is at the bottom of the uncanny valley, our sensation of strangeness grows quite large}''. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``a corpse, a zombie and a prosthetic hand''} $\leftarrow$ ``the \textbf{prosthetic hand is at the bottom of the valley}''; ``We might be happy this line is \textbf{into the still valley of a corpse} and that of not \textbf{the living dead}!'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``Each closely resembles a human at first glance.''} $\leftarrow$ ``recently prosthetic hands have improved greatly, and \textbf{we cannot distinguish them from real hands at a glance}''. \quad \textbf{(5)} \emph{``The eerie response arises only once the discrepancy registers.''} $\leftarrow$ ``this kind of prosthetic hand is too real and \textbf{when we notice it is prosthetic, we have a sense of strangeness}''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/uncanny1970.pdf}, \S\S``Valley of familiarity, ``The effects of movement and ``The significance of the uncanny. \textbf{Two scope notes.} (1) Mori writes ``\textbf{the living dead}, never ``zombie; zombie is the label on the reproduced Figure~1 and is standard usage in this literature. (2) The \textbf{corpse sits on the still curve and the living dead on the moving curve}, so they are not two points on one line. The thesis says only that Mori placed them inside the valley, which holds for each curve. The final sentence of the block (``Hence the place where that drop occurs\ldots) is the thesis authors\textquotesingle{} inference and correctly carries no citation.}

\citentry*
  {macdorman2006 --- MacDorman \& Ishiguro (2006)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation --- method]} Morphed image sequences ran from a mechanical humanoid robot, through an android, to a photograph of the person the android was modelled on. An android in this sense is a robot designed to be indistinguishable from a human in appearance and behaviour, as shown in Figure~3.2. Forty-five participants rated all thirty-one images on a nine-point scale of human likeness and a nine-point scale of familiarity. \textbf{They then marked the images that felt eerie and rated those on a ten-point scale}~\textbf{[cite]}.}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``ran from a mechanical humanoid robot, through an android, to a photograph of the person the android was modelled on''} $\leftarrow$ [\S The uncanny valley, p.~304:] ``two sets of \textbf{11 morphed images}\ldots a photograph of the \textbf{humanoid robot Qrio} (left) was morphed into a photograph of \textbf{an android}\ldots based on the science fiction author Philip K. Dick (center), which in turn was morphed into a photograph of \textbf{the author himself} (right)\ldots the \textbf{humanoid robot Eveliee} (left) was morphed into\ldots the \textbf{android Repliee Q1Expo} (center), which in turn was morphed into \textbf{Repliee's human model} (right).'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``a robot designed to be indistinguishable from a human in appearance and behaviour''} $\leftarrow$ [p.~298:] ``An android is defined to be `an artificial system designed with the ultimate goal of being \textbf{indistinguishable from humans in its external appearance and behavior}'.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``Forty-five participants rated all thirty-one images''} $\leftarrow$ [\S Participants, p.~305:] ``There were \textbf{45} Indonesian participants, 37 male and 8 female''; [\S Procedure, p.~306:] ``to rate \textbf{31 images} presented in random order''. \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``a nine-point scale of human likeness and a nine-point scale of familiarity''} $\leftarrow$ [p.~306:] ``on a \textbf{nine-point scale of human likeness and familiarity} --- that is, a scale ranging from very mechanical to very human and ranging from very strange to very familiar, \textbf{respectively}.'' \quad \textbf{(5)} \emph{``marked the images that felt eerie and rated those on a ten-point scale''} $\leftarrow$ [p.~306:] ``participants were asked, `\textbf{Which of the following figures gives you an eerie feeling?}' \textbf{The selected images were then presented} in random order and the participants were asked to rate how eerie each image was on a \textbf{ten-point scale}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/MacDorman2006AndroidScience.pdf}, pp.~298 and 304--306. \textbf{Each numbered pair maps one thesis claim to the passage supporting it.} \textbf{REVISED 2026-08-23:} the sentence previously said ``every image, which reads as the 22 morphs. It now states \textbf{thirty-one}, the paper\textquotesingle{}s own figure. The paper does not itemise the remaining nine, so the thesis does not characterise them.}

\citentry*
  {macdorman2006 --- MacDorman \& Ishiguro (2006)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation --- results]} Familiarity fell into a valley in both sequences and its trough lay between the humanoid robot and the android. Perceived human likeness rose steadily across the same span while eeriness peaked close to where familiarity was lowest. \textbf{This study reproduced Mori's observations from measured data}~\textbf{[cite]}. \emph{[Consequently, any near human avatar risks the same response if its rendering places it in that trough. --- thesis inference, uncited: the paper studies robots and androids, never avatars.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``Familiarity fell into a valley in both sequences and its trough lay between the humanoid robot and the android.''} $\leftarrow$ [\S Results, p.~306:] ``The results show a \textbf{valley} in the strange versus familiar scale\ldots \textbf{The trough of the valley occurs in both sets of morphed photographs between the humanoid robot and the android robot.}'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``Perceived human likeness rose steadily across the same span''} $\leftarrow$ ``In the region of the valley in both sets, there is a \textbf{monotonic increase in perceived human likeness} moving from the humanoid robot to the android to the human.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``eeriness peaked close to where familiarity was lowest''} $\leftarrow$ ``\textbf{The peak of the eeriness ratings roughly matches the trough of the familiarity ratings}.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``This study reproduced Mori's observations from measured data.''} $\leftarrow$ [p.~307:] ``In sum, \textbf{the experiment reproduced Mori's observations on the uncanny valley with two different sets of morphed photographs}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/MacDorman2006AndroidScience.pdf}, \S Results, pp.~306--307. \textbf{ADDED 2026-08-23:} these result sentences previously carried \emph{no} citation --- the paragraph\textquotesingle{}s only one sat on the procedure sentence. \textbf{Hedge note:} the paper says the eeriness peak ``\emph{roughly} matches the familiarity trough, so the thesis was softened to ``close to where familiarity was lowest.}

\citentry*
  {mcdonnell2012 --- McDonnell et al. (2012)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} This risk became the subject of direct empirical study. McDonnell et al. investigated the perceptual consequences of render style choice directly. Separate groups of participants rated still images and video of the same animated character, rendered across 10 distinct styles spanning the range from photorealistic to fully stylised (). The results exhibited a valley-shaped pattern across appeal, reassurance and trustworthiness alike. Four of the most abstract styles and the two most realistic ones scored significantly higher on all three measures than styles in the mid realism range. The authors concluded that styles at both ends of that range are viable. Interestingly, cartoon styles were rated friendlier than realistic ones. \textbf{However, appeal does not follow from abstraction alone, since one of their abstract styles ranked among the least appealing.} \emph{[Taken together, these findings suggest that stylised rendering can avoid the perceptual and trust costs concentrated in the mid realism range without requiring full photorealistic fidelity. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``rendered across ten distinct styles''} $\leftarrow$ \S4: ``We created \textbf{11 rendering styles} in total''; \S5.1.1: ``we used the male model rendered in \textbf{10 styles}\ldots The eleventh style with animated wrinkles was not included.'' Every ANOVA is reported as $F(9,288)$, confirming ten tested conditions. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``valley-shaped pattern across appeal, reassurance and trustworthiness\ldots four of the most abstract styles and the two most realistic ones''} $\leftarrow$ \S5.1.3 Appeal: ``\textbf{four of the most abstract and the two most realistic styles\ldots significantly more appealing than all other styles} ($p<0.05$)''; Trustworthy: ``Finally, \textbf{the most realistic Humans, and all other Toons were rated as equally trustworthy, and more trustworthy than all other styles} ($p<0.004$ in all cases)''. Re-assuring: ``\textbf{As with the Appeal ratings, the most realistic and most abstract styles were considered the most re-assuring} (ToonPencil, ToonFlat, ToonShaded, ToonCG, HumanHQ1, HumanHQ2)''; significance on that scale is reported at the opposite end --- ``\textbf{HumanIll was rated as the most eerie, and significantly more so than all other styles} ($p<0.00004$)'', followed by HumanBasic, ToonBare and HumanSSS. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``Cartoon styles were rated friendlier than realistic ones.''} $\leftarrow$ \S6 Discussion: ``Cartoon characters were considered highly appealing\ldots \textbf{They were rated as more friendly than realistic styles} and therefore might be more appropriate for certain virtual interactions.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``one of their abstract styles ranked among the least appealing''} $\leftarrow$ \S6: ``\textbf{However, we cannot say that all abstract cartoon characters are considered appealing. One of our render styles (ToonBare) was considered quite unappealing} and evoked negative reactions.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/ McDonnell, R., Breidt, M., and Bulthoff, H. H. (2012). Render me Real? Investigating.pdf}, \S\S4, 5.1.1, 5.1.2, 5.1.3 and 6. \textbf{Ten versus eleven --- know this one:} \S5.1.1 (still images) used \textbf{ten} styles, but \S5.1.2 (movies) states the motion was ``rendered in the \textbf{full 11 styles}. Every reported ANOVA is $F(9,288)$, that is ten levels, so the eleventh (animated wrinkles) was excluded from analysis. Ten therefore matches what was tested, analysed and shown in Figure~3.3, but a reader checking \S5.1.2 may query it. \textbf{Apparent conflict, resolved:} \S5.1.3 also says ``the most realistic render styles were rated as more friendly than HumanSSS and ToonBare. That is a narrower comparison against two specific low-scoring styles; the same passage then reports that \textbf{the Toons were rated the most friendly} ($p<0.009$), and \S6 states the overall result plainly. The thesis sentence follows \S6.}

Top row, from left to right: Toon Pencil, Toon Flat, Toon Shaded, Toon Bare and Toon CG. \textbf{Bottom row: Human Ill, Human Basic, Human SSS, Human HQ1 and Human HQ2.}}

Bottom row: Human Ill, Human Basic, Human SSS, Human HQ1 and Human HQ2''} $\leftarrow$ all ten names appear verbatim in \S4 and \S5.1.3. HumanIll; 5. HumanBasic, HumanSSS; 6. HumanHQ1, HumanHQ2}'', and by \S5.1.1: ``the \textbf{ToonPencil style was rated as the most abstract} (1.5 on the scale), and \textbf{HumanHQ2 was considered very realistic} (6 on the scale)''.}

Investigating.pdf}, Figure~3 caption and \S\S4, 5.1.1, 5.1.3. \textbf{Ten versus eleven:} the paper created eleven styles but tested ten --- \S4: ``\emph{Figure 3 depicts the first 10 styles, and Figure 9 shows an example of the eleventh style under motion}; \S5.1.1: ``\emph{The eleventh style with animated wrinkles was not included}. The thesis figure reproduces the ten-style Figure~3, so ``ten is correct in both the caption and the prose.}

\citentry*
  {dill2012 --- Dill et al. (2012)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Furthermore, Dill et al. validated the uncanny valley response specifically for CG characters drawn from contemporary commercial films and games. Their familiarity ratings followed Mori's predicted curve, with the strongest negative response in the near-human range. Motion deepened the valley most visibly for two of the ten characters tested. \textbf{Participants also named the eyes and the mouth as the facial regions producing the most discomfort.} \emph{[This confirmation that the valley extends to digital characters from popular media, not only to physical robots or androids, motivates its consideration across all virtual avatar contexts. Modern avatar systems fall within this same problematic region of the uncanny valley curve. They achieve enough visual realism to raise viewers' expectations, but cannot fully deliver on them in motion timing, expressions or gaze behaviour. As a result, a photorealistic avatar may undermine the very trustworthiness it was designed to convey. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``validated the uncanny valley response specifically for CG characters drawn from contemporary commercial films and games''} $\leftarrow$ [\S1, p.~511:] ``we selected \textbf{ten representative characters from recent and well known movies and games}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``familiarity ratings followed Mori's predicted curve, with the strongest negative response in the near-human range''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2, p.~511:] ``It follows the original chart proposed by Mori, where the horizontal axis represents the \textbf{human likeness} and the vertical axis indicates the \textbf{familiarity level}\ldots samples C.11, C.16, C.1 and C.14 suggest the same pattern of a \textbf{negative emotional response} as in result of an `\textbf{almost human but unnatural}' character representation.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``Motion deepened the valley most visibly for two of the ten characters tested.''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2, p.~512:] ``more noticeable in characters \textbf{C.16 and C.14 that exaggerated the valley in the video's curve}.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``named the eyes and the mouth as the facial regions producing the most discomfort''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2, p.~512:] ``The questionnaire also asked to the participants \textbf{which parts of the face} the participant felt more discomfort\ldots we could \textbf{highlight two specific parts of the face: eyes and mouth}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/Evaluation of the Uncanny Valley in CG Characters.pdf}, printed pp.~511--512 (PDF pp.~526--527 of the IVA 2012 volume). \textbf{Validate is the term the authors themselves use} --- \S3 Final Considerations states that it was possible to \emph{validate the uncanny valley persistence using a contemporary scenario}. \textbf{Evidentiary weight --- know this one:} the source is a three-page \emph{poster} (pp.~511--513). It reports \textbf{no participant count and no statistics}: no $N$, no $p$ values and no significance tests anywhere in it. All four thesis sentences are descriptive and none of them claims significance, so they match what the paper supports, but the source carries less weight than a full study. \textbf{Scope note:} participants were asked only about \emph{parts of the face}, so the paper supports non-uniformity within the face and says nothing about the rest of the body. The three sentences following the citation are the thesis authors\textquotesingle{} extrapolation to modern avatar systems and correctly carry no citation.}

\citentry*
  {seymour2021 --- Seymour et al. (2021)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Conversely, Seymour et al. tested that prediction directly using a professionally produced, real-time photorealistic avatar in a field study at a conference. None of the 18 guests who interacted with it displayed an aversive response. \textbf{Observers rated it as more trustworthy than a caricature counterpart, with greater affinity and stronger preference.} \emph{[This result suggests that a sufficiently high-fidelity, real-time avatar can escape the uncanny valley entirely rather than merely offset its cost with other advantages. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``a professionally produced, real-time photorealistic avatar in a field study at a conference''} $\leftarrow$ Abstract: ``We used a \textbf{field study}\ldots collected survey data from \textbf{SIGGRAPH conference} attendees\ldots \textbf{human-realistic avatars rendered in real time}.'' \S3.1: ``high fidelity facial input data, provided by \textbf{industry partner Cubic Motion}\ldots a custom facial rig developed by another partner, \textbf{3lateral}''. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``None of the eighteen guests who interacted with it displayed an aversive response.''} $\leftarrow$ Results: ``All guests made eye contact with the Digital MIKE, talked, interacted, and matched verbal cues. \textbf{None of the 18 guests displayed any type of aversive response}, and none ended the discussion prematurely (see Table 5).'' Table~5: Uncanniness of Digital MIKE --- \textbf{0 of 18}. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``Observers rated it as more trustworthy than a caricature counterpart, with greater affinity and stronger preference.''} $\leftarrow$ Abstract: ``\textbf{The observers} had positive evaluations of both avatars\ldots \textbf{They rated the human-realistic avatar as more trustworthy, had more affinity for it, and preferred it as a virtual agent}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/Seymour, Yuan, Dennis \& Riemer, "Have We Crossed the Uncanny Valley?" (2021).pdf}. \textbf{CORRECTED 2026-08-17.} The count of eighteen is confirmed by Table~5. Earlier wording attributed the affinity, trustworthiness and preference ratings to the same eighteen guests who interacted; the abstract assigns those to the \emph{observers}, a separate group. The thesis sentence now separates the two, which is why pair (2) and pair (3) name different groups. \textbf{Two further details.} (a) The thesis says \emph{a caricature counterpart}, singular, but there were \textbf{eighteen} of them: \S3.1 states that each guest was represented by a unique caricature avatar custom designed to be a caricature of that guest, so eighteen different caricature avatars were used against one constant human-realistic avatar. The comparison itself is unaffected. (b) Unlike the Dill poster, this study reports inferential statistics throughout, and the abstract records that \textbf{effect sizes ranged from medium to large}.}

\citentry*
  {tao2025 --- Tao et al. (2025)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ The Uncanny Valley and Avatar Realism}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} A larger and more recent test of that possibility comes from a network meta-analysis by Tao et al. It pooled 13 independent studies and 2,343 participants across three realism levels. High realism avatars were rated significantly more trustworthy than low realism avatars, whereas medium realism differed significantly from neither. \textbf{The cumulative ranking placed medium realism as the most eerie, although none of the eeriness comparisons reached significance.} \emph{[The pooled evidence therefore favours realism for trust and leaves the eeriness pattern unresolved. Overall, the valley reproduces reliably across robots, morphed photographs and CG characters, yet these studies disagree on whether stylisation or full realism is the safer route around it. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``a network meta-analysis by Tao et al.''} $\leftarrow$ Abstract: ``A \textbf{network meta-analysis (NMA)} was conducted.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``pooled 13 independent studies and 2,343 participants across three realism levels''} $\leftarrow$ Abstract: ``A total of \textbf{13 studies with 2,343 participants} were included in the analysis''; \S1: ``VAAR is categorized into three levels: (1) \textbf{high realism avatars (HRA)}, (2) \textbf{medium realism avatars (MRA)} and (3) \textbf{low realism avatars (LRA)}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``High realism avatars were rated significantly more trustworthy than low realism avatars, whereas medium realism differed significantly from neither.''} $\leftarrow$ \S3.6: ``The NMA results indicated that the \textbf{HRA received significantly higher ratings than the LRA} ($p = 0.046$). \textbf{No significant differences were observed between the MRA group and either the HRA or LRA groups}.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``None of the eeriness comparisons reached significance, although the cumulative ranking placed medium realism as the most eerie.''} $\leftarrow$ \S3.7: ``although the MRA ($p = 0.433$) and the HRA ($p = 0.766$) tended to be better than the LRA, these differences were \textbf{not statistically significant} at the $\alpha = 0.05$ level. \textbf{No significant difference was observed between the MRA and HRA either}''; and the SUCRA ranking: ``\textbf{MRA had the highest probability of being perceived as the most eerie (74.5\%)}, followed by HRA (44.7\%) and LRA (30.9\%)''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/Tao-Impact.pdf}, Abstract and \S\S1, 3.6, 3.7. \textbf{RESOLVED 2026-08-23:} the citation previously closed on the trustworthiness sentence, leaving the eeriness sentence, which is equally a Tao finding, outside it. The citation now closes the whole findings block and covers pairs (2) to (4). \textbf{The paper contradicts itself on eeriness --- know this one.} It asserts an MRA eeriness effect in \textbf{two} places while its own Results report the comparisons as null. \emph{Abstract:} ``In contrast, \textbf{medium VAAR elicited the strongest feelings of eeriness}. \emph{\S4.3 Eeriness, in the Discussion:} ``Our results showed that \textbf{MRA received higher eeriness ratings compared to HRA and LRA}, which suggests that they are \textbf{most likely to induce feelings of unease}. \emph{\S3.7, the only place statistics are reported:} the MRA ($p = 0.433$) and HRA ($p = 0.766$) differences from LRA were \textbf{not statistically significant}, and \textbf{no significant difference was observed between MRA and HRA either}. The assertive wording therefore rests on the \textbf{SUCRA probability ranking} (MRA most eerie at 74.5\%), not on a significance test. \textbf{The thesis follows \S3.7} and separates the ranking from the significance, which is the accurate reading. A reader who checks only the abstract or the discussion will think the thesis disagrees with the paper; in fact the paper disagrees with itself. The closing sentence of the block is the thesis synthesis and correctly carries no citation.}

\citentry*
  {mayer1995 --- Mayer et al. (1995)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Trust in virtual avatars is multidimensional, not a single attitude. Mayer et al. established this structure by decomposing perceived trustworthiness into ability, benevolence and integrity. \textbf{They further separated trustworthiness as an attribute of the trustee from trust as an attitude held by the trustor.}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``decomposing perceived trustworthiness into ability, benevolence and integrity''} $\leftarrow$ [p.~717:] ``Even though a number of factors have been proposed, \textbf{three characteristics of a trustee appear often in the literature: ability, benevolence, and integrity. As a set, these three appear to explain a major portion of trustworthiness}.'' Each is then defined in turn --- [p.~717:] ``\textbf{Ability} is that group of skills, competencies, and characteristics that enable a party to have influence within some specific domain''; [p.~718:] ``\textbf{Benevolence} is the extent to which a trustee is believed to want to do good to the trustor, aside from an egocentric profit motive''; [p.~719:] ``The relationship between \textbf{integrity} and trust involves the trustor's perception that the trustee adheres to a set of principles that the trustor finds acceptable.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``separated trustworthiness as an attribute of the trustee from trust as an attitude held by the trustor''} $\leftarrow$ trust is defined as the \emph{trustor's} state [p.~712:] ``the definition of trust proposed in this research is \textbf{the willingness of a party to be vulnerable to the actions of another party} based on the expectation that the other will perform a particular action important to the trustor''; the three trustee characteristics are explicitly \emph{not} that state [p.~717:] ``\textbf{Although they are not trust per se}, these variables help build the foundation for the development of trust''; and the separation is stated outright [p.~724:] ``\textbf{The distinction between a trustor's characteristics and trustee's characteristics is important.} Perceptions of ability, benevolence, and integrity of another party leave a considerable amount of variance in trust unexplained, because they neglect between-trustor differences in propensity to trust.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Mayer-IntegrativeModelOrganizational-1995.pdf}. Page numbers confirmed against the running heads in the JSTOR scan (\emph{Academy of Management Review} 20(3), 709--734). \textbf{REPLACED 2026-08-23:} the earlier excerpt quoted only ``three characteristics of the trustee that determine trustworthiness are examined'' --- it never named ability, benevolence and integrity, and left the trustor/trustee separation implicit. The passages below state both claims directly.}

\citentry*
  {mcknight2001 --- McKnight \& Chervany (2001)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{McKnight and Chervany subsequently carried that distinction into a typology separating trusting beliefs from trusting intentions in technology-mediated contexts.} \emph{[Both frameworks are set out in . Their consequence for the present review is that trust should not be reduced to a single score, as rendering style could plausibly affect ability, benevolence and integrity differently. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``a typology separating trusting beliefs from trusting intentions''} $\leftarrow$ [p.~13:] ``\textbf{Trusting Beliefs} means one believes that the other party has one or more characteristics beneficial to one''; [p.~14:] ``\textbf{Trusting Intentions} means one is willing to depend on, or intends to depend on, the other party even though one cannot control that party.'' The abstract lists them as separate conceptual constructs [p.~2:] ``The conceptual-level constructs consist of Disposition to Trust, Institution-based Trust, and \textbf{Trusting Beliefs and Trusting Intentions}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``carried that distinction''} --- the Mayer split between what is believed about the \emph{trustee} and what the \emph{trustor} is willing to do is preserved and made explicit [p.~14:] ``The link between Trusting Beliefs and Trusting Intentions is natural because the theory of reasoned action posits that \textbf{beliefs influence intentions}\ldots strong beliefs that the vendor is \textbf{honest, competent, benevolent, and predictable} should provide one the assurance to be \textbf{willing to depend} on them.'' The belief content maps onto Mayer's integrity, ability and benevolence, and the paper engages Mayer et al.\ directly elsewhere (pp.~5 and 8). \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``in technology-mediated contexts''} $\leftarrow$ [p.~13:] ``\textbf{In the Internet context}, the people involved include consumers and E-vendors''; [p.~14:] ``\textbf{In the Internet setting}\ldots the consumer has less control than in the brick-and-mortar context.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/What Trust Means in E-Commerce Customer Relationships.pdf}, pp.~2, 13--14. \textbf{Verification note --- this one is harder to check.} The PDF is a \textbf{scanned image with no text layer}: text search returns nothing and no fonts are embedded. The passages above were read from the rendered page images, and the Trusting Intentions definition on p.~14 was confirmed against the page image directly. \textbf{REPLACED 2026-08-23:} the earlier excerpt gave the two definitions but nothing supporting either ``carried that distinction'' from Mayer or ``technology-mediated contexts. Pairs (2) and (3) below supply both.}

\citentry*
  {alipour2025 --- Alipour et al. (2025)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Trust measurement has since been examined more broadly across the literature. Alipour et al. conducted a systematic review of 53 empirical studies to determine whether and how the uncanny valley effect influences trust. They found three different operationalisations of trust across this literature. The first is a direct rating of trust or trustworthiness. The second is a related construct such as competence or credibility. The third is a trust-related intention such as advice following. \textbf{The reported relationship between the uncanny valley effect and trust was inconsistent across these studies, with some reporting a direct negative effect and others finding no relationship at all.} \emph{[This inconsistency suggests that the effect of appearance on trust may depend on other perceptual factors. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``a systematic review of 53 empirical studies to determine whether and how the uncanny valley effect influences trust''} $\leftarrow$ [Introduction:] ``we conducted a \textbf{systematic review} of empirical research following the PRISMA protocol. From an initial pool of 311 records, 107 studies were screened in full, and \textbf{53 were included for detailed analysis}. Our review clarifies current knowledge on \textbf{how an agent's uncanniness, caused by the UVE and an almost humanlike appearance, affects trust}, and under which conditions.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``three different operationalisations of trust\ldots a direct rating\ldots a related construct such as competence or credibility\ldots a trust-related intention such as advice following''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.3.2:] ``Studies were categorized into \textbf{three groups} based on the type of trust measurement that they employed. [The first] consisted of studies that \textbf{directly and explicitly measured trust or trustworthiness} empirically\ldots The second group ($n = 11$) included studies that assessed \textbf{trust-related constructs, such as competence, acceptability, credibility, believability, and persuasiveness}. The third group ($n = 18$) contained studies that assessed \textbf{trust-related intentions, such as adherence to an agent's advice}, willingness to interact, or conformity to an agent's recommendation.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``inconsistent across these studies, with some reporting a direct negative effect and others finding no relationship at all''} $\leftarrow$ [\S5.1:] ``while the UVE affects trust, this relationship is often \textbf{not straightforward}. \textbf{Only about half of the studies}\ldots reported a \textbf{direct effect}. Moreover, slightly more than half of the studies reported \textbf{conditional or indirect effects}''; and the counts in [\S4.4.1:] ``Among the 41 coded studies, \textbf{19} were categorized as reporting a direct effect, \textbf{24} conditional or indirect effects, and \textbf{10 no effect}.'' The direction is the paper's own: it refers throughout to ``the UVE's \textbf{negative effect}\ldots on trust''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/alipour Would you rely on an eerie agent?.pdf}, Introduction and \S\S4.3.2, 4.4.1, 5.1. \textbf{REPLACED 2026-08-23.} The earlier excerpt for this source quoted only ``most studies rely on static images or hypothetical scenarios with limited real-time interaction'' --- a remark about study limitations, which supported none of the claims in this block. \textbf{Citation moved in the same pass:} the citation previously closed on the first sentence, leaving the taxonomy and the inconsistency finding, five sentences of Alipour results, with no support. It now closes the whole findings block. The final sentence of the paragraph is the thesis inference and correctly carries no citation.}

\citentry*
  {canales2024 --- Canales et al. (2024)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{The three avatar stylisation levels used by Canales et al.: realistic (AF-Real), semi-realistic (AM-Mid) and caricatured (CM-Caricature).}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the three levels and their labels} $\leftarrow$ [Figure~1 caption:] ``Images from our VR study showing the \textbf{three identities (AF, AM, CM) and styles (Real, Mid, Caricature)}.'' Each doctor is a distinct identity shown at one style level, which is why the thesis caption pairs them as AF-Real, AM-Mid and CM-Caricature. [\S3:] ``Each doctor is a different person (varied identity) and each is represented at a \textbf{different level of stylization: realistic, caricature, or a mid condition intended to be in-between realistic and caricature}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Avatar\_Stylization\_and\_Trust\_IEEE\_VR\_2024.pdf}, Figure~1 caption and \S3. Confirms both the identity labels and the style labels, and that identity is confounded with style level by design, which the paper acknowledges as a trade-off for ecological validity.}

\citentry*
  {nowak2003 --- Nowak \& Biocca (2003)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} One such factor is anthropomorphism. Nowak and Biocca manipulated both the anthropomorphism of a partner's virtual image and its perceived agency. Of these two factors, only anthropomorphism affected presence. Its effect also ran counter to their own prediction, because the less anthropomorphic image produced more copresence and social presence than the highly anthropomorphic one. \textbf{The authors attributed this to expectation, since a more anthropomorphic image raises what the viewer anticipates and presence falls when the interaction does not meet it.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``manipulated both the anthropomorphism of a partner's virtual image and its perceived agency''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``The experiment varied the level of anthropomorphism of the image of interactants: high anthropomorphism, low anthropomorphism, or no image. \textbf{Perceived agency was manipulated by telling the participants that the image was either an avatar controlled by a human, or an agent controlled by a computer}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``only anthropomorphism affected presence''} $\leftarrow$ [\S6.1, section heading:] ``\textbf{Agency Does Not Influence Presence}''; and Table~2, where the virtual image is significant on all three measures while agency is significant on none: perceived other's copresence \textbf{.22*} versus $-$.034, self-reported copresence \textbf{.17*} versus n.s., social presence \textbf{$-$.19*} versus $-$.09. [\S5:] ``The effect of an anthropomorphic virtual image on perceived other's copresence is \textbf{significant: $R = .22$, $F = 6.52$, $p = .01$}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``ran counter to their own prediction''} $\leftarrow$ Hypothesis~V predicted the reverse: ``People will feel \textbf{less} copresence with partners represented by \textbf{low}-anthropomorphic images than with partners represented by high-anthropomorphic images or by no images.'' Result: ``\textbf{This hypothesis was not supported. The effect of the low-anthropomorphic image was significant in the opposite direction}.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``the less anthropomorphic image produced more copresence and social presence''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``Participants interacting with the \textbf{less-anthropomorphic image reported more copresence and social presence} than those interacting with partners represented by either no image at all or by a highly anthropomorphic image.'' \quad \textbf{(5)} \emph{``The authors attribute this to expectation\ldots presence falls when the interaction does not meet it''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``indicating that the more anthropomorphic images \textbf{set up higher expectations that lead to reduced presence when these expectations were not met}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Nowak2003.pdf}, Abstract, \S5 Results, Table~2 and \S6.1. \textbf{REVISED 2026-08-23.} Two changes. First, the earlier support showed only that \emph{agency} was null, which does not establish that anthropomorphism had an effect. Table~2 supplies the positive evidence: anthropomorphism is significant on all three presence measures while agency is significant on none. Second, the thesis previously explained the reversal as diminishing perceptual returns, which is not the mechanism the paper proposes. It now reports the authors own explanation, expectation violation, which also matches the mechanism behind the uncanny valley analogy drawn in the next sentence.}

\citentry*
  {nowak2005 --- Nowak \& Rauh (2005)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} This pattern is structurally analogous to the uncanny valley, where affinity likewise ceases to scale linearly with human resemblance and inverts before recovering at full fidelity. Nowak and Rauh later carried this question into credibility. They asked 255 participants to rate a series of avatars. \textbf{Avatars perceived as more anthropomorphic were also rated as more credible and were chosen more often for self-representation.} \emph{[Therefore, avatar design choices function as meaningful social signals. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``Avatars perceived as more anthropomorphic were also rated as more credible and were chosen more often for self-representation''} $\leftarrow$ [Conclusion, JCMC p.~172:] ``\textbf{Avatars that were more anthropomorphic were perceived to be more attractive and credible, and people were more likely to choose to be represented by them.}'' Corroborated by Table~3: anthropomorphism $\times$ credibility $r = .46$, $p < .01$. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the 255 participants} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``In this study, \textbf{participants (N = 255)} evaluated a series of avatars\ldots in terms of their androgyny, anthropomorphism, credibility, homophily, attraction, and the likelihood they would choose them during an interaction.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Nowak2005.pdf}, Abstract, Results RQ8, Table~3 and Conclusion. \textbf{THREE ERRORS FOUND AND CORRECTED 2026-08-25, the most serious in Ch.~3.} The passage previously read ``Both anthropomorphism \emph{and gender expression} predicted the resulting credibility ratings. More anthropomorphic avatars were rated as more credible. The authors noted that \emph{credibility is itself among the primary predictors of trust}.'' Each of the three claims failed for a different reason. \textbf{(a) The gender half is contradicted by the source, not merely unsupported.} The RQ8 regression gives image gender $\to$ credibility $b = -.01$, $p = .55$, and the Discussion states it outright: ``masculine images\ldots child-like images\ldots \textbf{these characteristics did not significantly influence attributions of credibility}. The Results are equally explicit: ``\textbf{Credibility was negatively predicted only by} whether or not the image was an object ($b = -.33$) or animal ($b = -.28$). No weaker wording survives, so the clause was deleted rather than softened. \textbf{(b) ``Predicted'' overstated even the anthropomorphism half.} The paper's own Conclusion disclaims that reading: ``Previous research has implied that anthropomorphism would be \textbf{the main predictor of credibility} or attractiveness (Koda, 1996; Wexelblat, 1997), \textbf{but this was not the case in the present study}.'' The relation holds correlationally, which is how the sentence now states it, in the authors' own Conclusion wording. \textbf{(c) The trust sentence was deleted outright.} The word ``trust'' appears \textbf{three times in the entire article}: twice inside one background sentence that Nowak and Rauh credit to others, ``credibility is one of the primary predictors for certain interaction outcomes such as attitude change and trust\ldots (McCroskey \& Young, 1981)(and McCroskey, Hamilton \& Weiner, 1974), and once in a reference title. \textbf{They never measured trust.} A reader would have taken a borrowed definitional aside for a finding of a 255-participant avatar study. Under \S5.3a the origin is McCroskey, which is not held, so the claim was removed rather than re-cited. \textbf{Nothing downstream breaks:} the bridge into the Canales paragraph is ``social signals'', not trust.}

\citentry*
  {alipour2025 --- Alipour et al. (2025)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Canales et al. tested this signalling role directly by manipulating avatar stylisation and measuring its effect on multi-component trust. Participants were presented with virtual doctors rendered at three stylisation levels: realistic, semi-realistic and caricatured. Trust was measured after each doctor interview, both as a direct rating and across the ability, benevolence and integrity dimensions. In addition, participants were asked which doctor they would consult for a follow-up. This provided a behavioural indicator alongside the self-report scale. \textbf{Together these span all three measurement approaches in the Alipour et al. taxonomy.}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``span all three measurement approaches in the Alipour et al.\ taxonomy''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.3.2:] ``Studies were categorized into \textbf{three groups} based on the type of trust measurement\ldots [the first] consisted of studies that \textbf{directly and explicitly measured trust or trustworthiness}\ldots The second group included studies that assessed \textbf{trust-related constructs, such as competence, acceptability, credibility}\ldots The third group contained studies that assessed \textbf{trust-related intentions, such as adherence to an agent's advice}.'' Canales et al.\ used all three: a direct trust rating, the ability, benevolence and integrity dimensions, and the choice of which doctor to consult.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/alipour Would you rely on an eerie agent?.pdf}, \S4.3.2. The thesis sentence maps the Canales measures onto the three Alipour groups, so the support is the taxonomy itself rather than a finding.}

\citentry*
  {canales2024 --- Canales et al. (2024)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The results indicated that self-reported trust ratings were broadly equivalent across all three style levels. \textbf{However, the authors found that the semi-realistic condition attracted the highest rate of behavioural selection.}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``virtual doctors rendered at three stylisation levels: realistic, semi-realistic and caricatured''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``Participants meet three different doctors with \textbf{three different style levels: realistic, caricatured, and an in-between `Mid' level}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``both as a direct rating and across the ability, benevolence and integrity dimensions''} $\leftarrow$ [\S1:] ``Our study employs the trust model proposed by Mayer et al.\ which posits that trust requires people to be vulnerable and that trusting another relies on positive perceptions of their \textbf{ability, benevolence and integrity}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``participants were asked which doctor they would consult for a follow-up''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] participants ``must imagine that they have received a cancer diagnosis and must \textbf{select a doctor for a second opinion}\ldots After interviewing three doctors, they selected [one].'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``self-reported trust ratings were broadly equivalent across all three style levels''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``\textbf{Trust ratings are largely consistent across the style levels}.'' \quad \textbf{(5)} \emph{``the semi-realistic condition attracted the highest rate of behavioural selection''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``but \textbf{participants were more likely to select doctors with the `Mid' level of stylization for a second opinion}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Avatar\_Stylization\_and\_Trust\_IEEE\_VR\_2024.pdf}, Abstract, Figure~1 and \S3. \textbf{CORRECTED 2026-08-23:} the thesis previously said trust was measured across the \emph{competence}, benevolence and integrity dimensions, and then added a sentence explaining that competence corresponds to what Mayer et al.\ term ability. Canales et al.\ do not use competence. They use Mayer own term, ability, 37 times against 3 for competence, and state the model as positive perceptions of ability, benevolence and integrity. The thesis now uses their term, and the competence mapping was moved to Methodology where that label first appears.}

\citentry*
  {gao2025 --- Gao et al. (2025)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Trust and Credibility in Virtual Agents}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} This suggests that moderate stylisation can match or exceed the behavioural trust of full photorealism. \textbf{Gao et al. compared a stylised agent against recorded video of the real speaker it was modelled on.} \emph{[The two did not differ on a direct trust rating, but the real speaker was rated higher on ability, benevolence and integrity. Overall, these studies indicate that a single self-report rating does not fully capture how render style shapes trust. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``compared a stylised agent against recorded video of the real speaker it was modelled on''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.2:] ``\textbf{For the Real style, we directly played the corresponding video}''; the stimuli are three TED talks and ``\textbf{The three speakers of different ages correspond to the identities of the virtual agents} in our design.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``The two did not differ on a direct trust rating''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.2, Trust:] ``Real style was perceived as more trustworthy than No-stylization ($p = 0.002$), but \textbf{there was no significant difference in trustworthiness between Real and Stylization ($p = 0.216$)}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``the real speaker was rated higher on ability, benevolence and integrity''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.2:] ``\textbf{Real style was perceived as more competent than Stylization ($p < 0.001$)}''; ``\textbf{Real style was perceived as more benevolent than both Stylization ($p = 0.007$)}''; ``\textbf{Real style was perceived as more integrous than both Stylization ($p = 0.015$)}''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/IEEE\_VR25\_TVCG.pdf} (Gao et al., \emph{Trust in Virtual Agents: Exploring the Role of Stylization and Voice}), \S\S3.2 and 4.2. \textbf{REVISED 2026-08-23:} the sentence previously opened ``Gao et al.\ removed that restriction'', which pointed back at an Alimardani et al.\ sentence that has since been deleted. The opener is now self-contained. \textbf{Note the pattern:} trust as a single rating showed no difference between Real and Stylization, while all three component dimensions did. That dissociation is the same one this thesis relies on for measuring trust componentwise rather than as one score.}

They conducted a VR face-to-face interaction study and found that stylised avatars were rated significantly less eerie than photorealistic avatars. \textbf{Trustworthiness also trended higher for the stylised avatars, though this difference did not reach statistical significance.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``a VR face-to-face interaction study''} $\leftarrow$ [Title:] ``\textbf{Effect of avatar stylization and facial expression intensity in virtual interactions}'', \emph{Virtual Reality} 29:163 (2025). \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``stylised avatars were rated significantly less eerie than photorealistic avatars''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.2.3:] ``The results show that the stylized character ($m = 2.750$, $SD = 1.256$, \textbf{$p = .05$}), and the semi-realistic character ($m = 2.650$, $SD = 1.331$, \textbf{$p = .026$}) were \textbf{perceived as significantly less eerie than the realistic character} ($m = 3.350$, $SD = 1.703$).'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``Trustworthiness also trended higher\ldots though this difference did not reach statistical significance''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.2:] ``The results suggest, \textbf{as a trend}, that the stylized character ($m = 5.450$) and the semi-realistic character ($m = 5.475$) were judged as more trustworthy than the realistic character ($m = 4.900$), \textbf{$p = .075$}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Effect\_of\_avatar\_stylization\_and\_facial\_expression.pdf}, \S4.2. \textbf{Scope note:} the \emph{main effect} of stylisation on trustworthiness \emph{was} significant ($F(2,74) = 3.326$, $p = .041$, $\eta^2 = .043$); it is the \emph{pairwise} comparison that did not reach significance ($p = .075$). The thesis sentence is correct about the comparison, but a reader may take it as meaning nothing was significant.}

\citentry*
  {kumar2019 --- Pavan Kumar et al. (2019)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} NPR transforms an image into abstracted, artistic or stylised output instead of reproducing it exactly. \textbf{Pavan Kumar et al. surveyed these approaches under four established categories: stroke-based rendering, image analogy, region-based techniques and image filtering.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``four categories: stroke-based rendering, image analogy, region-based techniques and image filtering''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2 Classifications of NPR techniques:] ``NPR techniques have been classified to fall into following categories\ldots \textbf{1. Stroke-based rendering. 2. Image analogy. 3. Region-based techniques. 4. Image processing and filtering techniques.}'' The abstract lists the same four: ``the various works done on \textbf{stroke-based rendering, color image analogy, region-based rendering, image filtering abstraction, and stylization}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Kumar- A comprehensive survey on NPR.pdf}, \S2 and Abstract. \textbf{Two notes.} (a) Kumar names the fourth family \emph{image processing and filtering techniques}; the thesis shortens this to \emph{image filtering} and uses that shorthand consistently. (b) Kumar does not originate the taxonomy. The same passage states that the categories are given ``\emph{based on the work entitled State of the Art: a taxonomy of artistic stylization techniques for images and video}'' and two further sources, so Kumar is adopting and surveying an existing classification rather than proposing one. The thesis verb \emph{classified} slightly overstates that contribution; \emph{group} or \emph{adopt} would be exact.}

\citentry*
  {kumar2019 --- Pavan Kumar et al. (2019)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Stroke-based rendering builds an image from discrete brush strokes that vary in size, colour, texture and orientation. Placing these strokes typically requires a dedicated analysis pass to decide where and how each stroke should be oriented. That requirement is a drawback for pipelines with only one shading stage per pixel. Image analogy instead transfers colour and texture style from an example pair of source and target images onto a new image. This transfer depends on a precomputed exemplar or a patch-matching search rather than information available within a single frame. By comparison, region-based techniques segment an image into distinct regions before abstracting each one individually. Segmentation is an iterative process that considers the whole image. Its result cannot be computed within a single, independently executed per-pixel operation. \textbf{Finally, image filtering applies local mathematical operations, such as gradients or bilateral smoothing, directly to the image to suppress detail while preserving structure.} \emph{[These operations examine only a small neighbourhood around each pixel. As a result, they remain computable within a single shading pass. summarises this classification. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{stroke-based rendering} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.1:] the family is built from discrete brush strokes whose placement requires a dedicated analysis stage. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{image analogy} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.2:] style is transferred from an example pair of source and target images onto a new image. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{region-based techniques} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.3:] the image is segmented into regions that are then abstracted individually. \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{image filtering} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.4 Image processing and filtering:] ``\textbf{Image processing and filtering techniques are used to develop image abstraction and stylization}\ldots Image abstraction is the task of producing simplified images from given images by \textbf{removing unwanted details while retaining meaningful information} in it.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Kumar- A comprehensive survey on NPR.pdf}, \S\S2.1--2.4. \textbf{Citation added 2026-08-24.} The paragraph defines all four families in turn, but only the opening list carried a citation, so the four definitions that followed it stood bare. A second citation of the same source now closes the run of definitions. The sentences interleaved between them, each one drawing out what a family costs a single shading pass, are the thesis's own reasoning and are deliberately left uncited.}

\citentry*
  {lake2000 --- Lake et al. (2000)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} However, this survey's scope is limited to 2D image processing. Image filtering crosses that boundary where the other three do not, since each of them requires a pass that a single shading stage cannot provide. Other real-time techniques fall outside Pavan Kumar et al.'s four families, because they act on geometry and lighting rather than on a finished image. Toon shading is one such technique. It quantises the continuous diffuse lighting term into a small number of discrete bands. Consequently, this approach produces flat-shaded regions separated by hard edges and gives characters a cartoon appearance, as depicted in . \textbf{That separation depends on a single input, the lighting term.} \emph{[This dependency leaves the ramp unable to respond to any other surface property. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``quantises the continuous diffuse lighting term''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.1 Cartoon Shading:] the diffuse term of Equation~1, $(\max\{L\cdot n,0\})$, supplies the texture coordinate. Figure~2 caption: ``\textbf{Generation of texture coordinates from $L\cdot n$}. In this case, the shadow boundary occurs at the point where $L\cdot n$ equals 0.5.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``into a small number of discrete bands''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.1:] ``the cartoonist typically uses \textbf{solid colors that do not vary across the materials they represent}\ldots our hard shading technique \textbf{finds a transition boundary and shades each side of the boundary with a solid color}.'' The algorithm stores, for each material, ``a \textbf{one-dimensional texture map}'' built from ``a texture map of a \textbf{minimal number of colors}''. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``flat-shaded regions separated by hard edges\ldots a cartoon appearance''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.1:] ``The boundary between shadowed and illuminated colors is a \textbf{hard edge} that follows the contours of the object or character. We refer to this technique as \textbf{hard shading}.'' And: ``Our system allows a 3D model to be drawn to look \textbf{just like a traditional 2D cartoon}, and to be viewed interactively.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``That separation depends on a single input, the lighting term''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.1:] ``The boundary between lit and shadowed colors on the model \textbf{depends, just as the color calculated in smooth shading, on the cosine of the angle between the light and normal vectors}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/npar2000\_lake\_et\_al.pdf}, \S3.1 and Figure~2. \textbf{REPLACED 2026-08-24.} This entry previously read only ``See entry \#N for the source passage'', pointing at itself, so it carried no supporting quote at all. \textbf{Citation moved 2026-08-24:} it previously closed on the quantisation sentence, leaving the two sentences that follow, both Lake findings, without support. It now closes the block. \textbf{Overstatement corrected:} the thesis previously said artists have little independent influence over the boundary placement. Lake states the opposite, that a higher-resolution texture can provide flexibility in the location of the shadow boundary. The sentence now says the ramp cannot respond to any property other than the lighting, which is what the dependence on $L\cdot n$ implies and is the premise Barla et al. address.}

The flat bands separated by hard edges visible in the figure are exactly this effect.}

The second layer, P2, is made up of parts of \textbf{back facing polygons} (c). The \textbf{intersection of these two layers in image space creates silhouette edges} (d).''}

Each now has its own.}

  {barla2006 --- Barla et al. (2006)}

This enables a 2D lookup that encodes artistic intent unavailable in a 1D quantisation step. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``extended toon shading's quantisation into a richer parameter space''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``Traditional toon shading uses a \textbf{1D texture} that describes how tone varies with surface orientation relative to a given light source. In this paper we describe two extensions to the basic algorithm that support view-dependent effects.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``retains the lighting term on one axis\ldots adds a second axis for tone detail, driven by a view-dependent attribute such as depth or surface orientation''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``First, we \textbf{replace the 1D texture with a 2D texture whose second dimension corresponds to the desired ``tone detail,'' which can vary with depth or surface orientation}.'' The first axis is unchanged, since the conventional 1D texture it replaces is the one indexed by surface orientation relative to the light.}
