The thesis covers only the first, the 2D ramp. The second uses a modified normal to support backlighting and plastic or metallic highlights, and is not used in this work.}

\citentry*
  {raskar1999 --- Raskar \& Cohen (1999)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Silhouette rendering is a second such technique. It derives line structure from geometry rather than from the lighting term. Raskar and Cohen achieved this by processing the mesh twice from the same viewpoint. Initially, the front-facing polygons are drawn in the usual way. The back-facing polygons are then rendered and translated towards the camera. Thus, part of this back-facing geometry appears in front of the occluding front-facing surface and the visible rim left at this boundary forms the outline, as illustrates. \textbf{The outline emerges from geometry alone and requires no precomputed adjacency information.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the adjacency claim, stated by the authors as a headline property} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract, \textbf{PDF p.~1}, final sentence before \S1:] ``A solution is suggested using view-dependent modification of polygonal objects. \textbf{The method does not require any preprocessing or adjacency information and hence is applicable for dynamic scenes.}'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the same claim restated} $\leftarrow$ [\S1 Introduction, \textbf{PDF p.~1}:] ``The method is robust, general purpose and easy to implement. \textbf{No pre-processing or adjacency information is required.} This allows use of silhouette edges in \textbf{dynamic scenes, models with changing topology}\ldots'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that the outline is produced by geometry rather than by the shading term} $\leftarrow$ [\S3, algorithm listing:] the outline appears purely from rendering front faces, then back faces under front-face culling with an equal-to depth test. No edge detection step occurs anywhere in the algorithm.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Raskar -Image Precision Silhouette Edges.pdf}, PDF p.~1. \textbf{WARNING FOR ANYONE RE-CHECKING THIS: ``adjacency information'' appears four times on PDF p.~1 and only the first two are Raskar and Cohen's claim about their own method.} The other two describe other people's work: ``models with known adjacency information'' refers to \textbf{Markosian et al.}, a method that does require it, and ``The method in [Rossignac92] is image precision and does not need adjacency information'' refers to \textbf{Rossignac}. The abstract sentence in~(1) is the one to check against. \textbf{FALSE CLAIM REPLACED 2026-08-25, found by Nidanur.} The sentence previously read ``The outline emerges from geometry alone and \emph{requires no analysis of the rendered image}. That second half is not true of this method and no source supports it. Raskar and Cohen's paper is titled \emph{\textbf{Image Precision} Silhouette Edges}, its abstract describes computing surfaces ``\textbf{in image space} at interactive rates'' with ``all operations\ldots performed in \textbf{image-precision}'', and the outline only resolves because the depth buffer decides which fattened back-faces show through. Isenberg et al.\ file the whole family under \textbf{Hybrid algorithms}: ``operations in object space that are followed by rendering the modified polygons\ldots using the z-buffer\ldots The result is similar to image space algorithms.'' \textbf{Citation moved in the same pass.} It previously closed one sentence earlier. The adjacency claim is Raskar and Cohen's rather than the thesis's summary, so it cannot sit outside the cited block, and the citation now closes on it. The preceding sentence loses nothing: the figure it points to carries \texttt{raskar1999} in its own caption. \textbf{Why this fact rather than another.} Raskar and Cohen state it because no adjacency data means silhouettes work on ``dynamic scenes, models with changing topology''. That is exactly the constraint an animated avatar imposes, so the sentence now connects forward to the Methodology selection rationale instead of asserting a property the method does not have. \textbf{The same overreach was removed from Methodology on the same day.} V1 had read ``eliminating the need for edge detection or image-space analysis'', which duplicated the preceding sentence and made the same false claim. It was deleted, and the \texttt{isenberg2003} citation was removed from the neighbouring selection sentence, which is the thesis's own design decision and takes no citation under \S5.1 rule~5.}

\citentry*
  {isenberg2003 --- Isenberg et al. (2003)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} That outline is only one of several line types that convey shape. In order to detect those lines, Isenberg et al. surveyed the available algorithms and reported that the literature groups silhouette lines together with creases, borders and self-intersections under the heading of feature lines. \textbf{The silhouette lines themselves split further, and the survey calls the interior ones internal silhouettes.} \emph{[This distinction matters for avatar rendering, where expressive detail such as facial features, clothing folds and skin creases sits inside the outline. However, lines that appear only in the texture fall outside this classification, since each type it names is defined on the mesh geometry. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``grouped silhouette lines together with creases, borders and self-intersections under the heading of feature lines''} $\leftarrow$ [\S Definitions and terminology, journal p.~28:] ``Other lines significant in the context of silhouettes are \textbf{creases, borders, and self-intersections}\ldots In the literature, these three additional line categories \textbf{together with the silhouette lines are often called feature lines}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``The silhouette lines themselves split further, and the survey calls the interior ones internal silhouettes''} $\leftarrow$ [same section:] ``We define a \textbf{contour as the subset of the silhouette that separates the object from the background}. Also, \textbf{the subset of the silhouette, which in 2D divides one portion of the object from another one of the same object, is not part of the contour}. Those lines are sometimes called \textbf{internal silhouettes}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Isenberg\_2003\_ADG.pdf}, journal p.~28. \textbf{Error found and corrected 2026-08-24:} the thesis previously read ``Internal silhouettes \emph{and creases} fall inside the projected outline. The internal-silhouette half is a fair restatement of the source, but the crease half is not supported. Isenberg defines a crease purely by dihedral angle, ``edges that should always be drawn, for instance the edges of a cube'', and says nothing about where a crease lies relative to the projected outline. Several edges of a cube lie \emph{on} that outline, so the claim is not even generally true. \textbf{Also removed:} the separate sentence ``Only the contour separates the object from the background'', which restated the definition without adding anything. The contrast it carried is now implicit in ``the interior ones. \textbf{Citation moved in the same pass:} it previously closed on the feature-lines sentence, leaving two further Isenberg claims uncited after it. It now closes the block, and the final sentence about avatar rendering is the thesis's own inference and stays uncited. \textbf{Scope note:} the survey's three extra categories, creases, borders and self-intersections, are defined by geometric cause, not by position inside or outside the outline. Only the contour-versus-internal-silhouette split is an inside/outside one, and that one the source states explicitly. \textbf{Attribution corrected 2026-08-24, closing an item left open since the entry was first written.} The sentence read ``Isenberg et al.\ surveyed the available algorithms and \emph{grouped} silhouette lines\ldots under the heading of feature lines'', which presented the grouping as their own act of classification. The source says these categories ``\textbf{in the literature}\ldots \textbf{are often called} feature lines'', so the survey reports an existing convention rather than proposing one. The sentence now reads ``reported that the literature groups\ldots. This is the same correction already applied to Pavan Kumar et al., whose taxonomy is likewise stated to be based on three earlier works.}

\citentry*
  {isenberg2003 --- Isenberg et al. (2003)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The same survey identifies a further family of algorithms that addresses this limitation by working on the rasterised output rather than on the mesh. \textbf{These image-space methods, an instance of the image filtering category described above, extract discontinuities from the buffers that conventional rendering already produces and represent the result as features in a pixel matrix.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {``[Image space algorithms section, p.~29:] ``Image space algorithms exploit discontinuities in the image buffer(s) that result from conventional rendering and extract them using image-processing methods. These methods provide a silhouette represented as features in a pixel matrix.''''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Isenberg\_2003\_ADG.pdf}, p.~2 of the PDF (journal p.~29). Both halves of the thesis sentence map onto this passage: ``extract discontinuities from the buffers'' to the first source sentence, ``features in a pixel matrix'' to the second. \textbf{Scope note:} the following thesis sentences about edge thickness scaling with camera distance are deliberately left uncited. Isenberg does not make that claim anywhere in the survey. Its general stated disadvantage (p.~30) is that ``the user has little control over the resulting lines' attributes'', and the ``limitation to pixel accuracy'' remark (p.~35) concerns silhouette \emph{visibility} determination, not edge width. The distance-scaling behaviour is an observation from this project.}

\citentry*
  {saitotakahashi1990 --- Saito \& Takahashi (1990)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Saito and Takahashi introduced one such buffer, the G-buffer. It is a set of channels that preserves per-pixel geometric properties such as depth and surface normals. The authors recovered profile and internal edges from the depth channel. \textbf{Sobel was recommended for the profile, and a second-order operator produced the internal edges.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``profile and internal edges\ldots from the depth channel''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.2 Drawing Edges:] ``\textbf{profile} --- the border line of an object on the screen; \textbf{internal edge} --- a line where two faces meet. Profiles and edges are the \textbf{0-th and first order discontinuities of the depth image (sz image)} respectively.'' Restated in \S4: ``The \textbf{profile image (pr) was obtained from the sz image}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``Sobel was recommended for the profile''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.1:] ``Discontinuities of an image can be extracted with a \textbf{first order differential operator}\ldots however, \textbf{we recommend Sobel's}.'' Figure~3 shows the depth image passing through the first order differential to give the profile image. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``a second order operator produced the internal edges''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.1:] ``Discontinuities of the first order differentials of an image can be extracted with a \textbf{second order differential operator}. For this calculation, we recommend the following operator: $I = (8X - A - B - C - D - E - F - G - H)/3$.'' Figure~3 shows that branch producing the internal edge image.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Comprehensible Rendering of 3-D Shapes Saito.pdf}, \S\S3.1--3.2 and Figure~3. \textbf{Precision corrected 2026-08-24.} The sentence read ``recovered profile and internal edges from the depth channel and recommended the Sobel operator \emph{for the task}'', which reads as though Sobel produced both. It did not. Sobel is the first order operator and yields the \emph{profile}; the internal edges come from the second order operator of Eq.~2. The sentence was split so each operator is matched to the edge type it actually produces. \textbf{Passage not to confuse with these:} \S3.3, on contour lines of a scalar field, prefers its own Eq.~9 and states that an operator ``is recommended rather than the Sobel Operator. Different operation, no contradiction.}

\citentry*
  {nienhaus2003 --- Nienhaus \& D\"ollner (2003)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Nienhaus and D\"ollner later applied the same analysis to the normal channels and detected crease edges from their discontinuities in real time.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``applied the same analysis to the normal channels''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3:] ``A \textbf{normal-buffer is a three-channel image} containing encoding normalized x, y, and z coordinates of normals derived from fragments resulting from 3D scene objects.'' The lineage is stated in \S2: ``\textbf{Saito and Takahashi introduced the G-buffer}'', and \S4.1.2 reuses their operator: ``\textbf{Saito and Takahashi as well as Decaudin propose the following C1-discontinuity operator} to detect discontinuities in the z-buffer''. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``detected crease edges from their discontinuities''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3:] ``Abrupt changes in the normal-buffer occur typically at crease edges\ldots \textbf{0th order discontinuities of the normal-buffer indicate crease edges}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``in real time''} $\leftarrow$ [Title:] ``Edge-Enhancement --- \textbf{An Algorithm for Real-Time Non-Photorealistic Rendering}.'' [Abstract:] ``we propose an algorithm for enhancing edges of \textbf{real-time} non-photorealistic renderings.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Edge Enhancement.pdf}, \S\S2--4. \textbf{Scope note:} the same passage adds that normal-buffer discontinuities also indicate ``profile edges if the normal of the adjacent front-facing polygon varies from the normal of an underlying polygon'', so crease edges are the principal but not the only outcome. The thesis claims only the crease edges, so it is narrower than the source rather than broader, which is the safe direction.}

\citentry*
  {isenberg2003 --- Isenberg et al. (2003)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Isenberg et al. favoured the combination of both channels on visual grounds.}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``combining both channels yields a pleasing result''} $\leftarrow$ [journal p.~29:] ``Hertzmann extends this method by using a \textbf{normal buffer} instead. This can also detect C1 discontinuities. \textbf{Combining both approaches yields a pleasing result} (see Figure 2).'' [Figure~2 caption:] ``(a) z-buffer, (b) edges extracted from the z-buffer, (c) normal buffer, (d) edges extracted from the normal buffer, and \textbf{(e) combination of both edge buffers}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Isenberg\_2003\_ADG.pdf}, journal p.~29 and Figure~2. \textbf{Wording corrected twice, 2026-08-24:} the thesis first read ``the combination of both channels as the \emph{stronger result}'', a firmer judgement than the survey makes. The first correction swapped in the survey's own phrase, ``yields a pleasing result'', which fixed the overstatement but reproduced the source verbatim without quotation marks. The sentence now paraphrases instead: ``favour\ldots on visual grounds'' carries the same soft, aesthetic endorsement the survey makes, in the thesis's own words. \textbf{Attribution note:} the survey credits the normal-buffer extension to Hertzmann rather than to Nienhaus. This does not affect the preceding thesis sentence, since that one cites Nienhaus and D\"ollner's own paper for their own work.}

\citentry*
  {isenberg2003 --- Isenberg et al. (2003)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} However, neither channel registers a boundary that exists only as colour, since a change of pigment leaves depth and surface orientation alike untouched. \textbf{Colour-buffer edges are treated in the survey as a liability, because shading and texture variation cause detections that are not silhouettes.}}
  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``Colour-buffer edges are treated in the survey as a liability, because shading and texture variation cause detections that are not silhouettes''} $\leftarrow$ [\S Image space algorithms, journal p.~29:] ``The easiest way to find significant lines would be to \textbf{detect edges in the color buffer}. This, however, \textbf{doesn't necessarily detect silhouettes since changes in shading and texturing can cause erroneous edge detection}.'' The survey's preference follows immediately: ``Saito and Takahashi suggest \textbf{using the z-buffer instead}\ldots This has the advantage of \textbf{only finding object-relevant edges such as silhouette lines including contours}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Isenberg\_2003\_ADG.pdf}, journal p.~29. \textbf{REPLACED 2026-08-24.} This entry previously carried the pixel-matrix quote copied from the earlier image-space entry, which says nothing about the colour buffer and therefore did not support this sentence at all. The passage above is the one the sentence is actually drawn from, and it matches closely, including the shading-and-texturing cause.}

\citentry*
  {gonzalez2018 --- Gonzalez \& Woods (2018)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} That liability becomes both an objective and a challenge once the target is a stylised avatar rather than a silhouette. Clothing patterns, facial contours and painted surface detail are the features that need marking here. Detecting them in the colour channel requires a gradient operator. \textbf{The Sobel filter is preferable for that purpose, because its kernel suppresses noise better than the simpler alternatives.}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``requires a gradient operator''} $\leftarrow$ [\S10.2:] ``the three steps performed typically for edge detection are: 1.~Image smoothing for noise reduction\ldots 2.~\textbf{Detection of edge points}\ldots 3.~Edge localization.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``the Sobel filter''} $\leftarrow$ [Eqs.~10-24 and 10-25:] ``\textbf{These kernels are called the Sobel operators} (Sobel [1970]).'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``is preferable here, because its kernel suppresses noise better than the simpler alternatives''} $\leftarrow$ [same page:] ``The \textbf{Prewitt kernels are simpler to implement} than the Sobel kernels, but the slight computational difference between them typically is not an issue. The fact that the \textbf{Sobel kernels have better noise-suppression (smoothing) characteristics makes them preferable} because \ldots noise suppression is an important issue when dealing with derivatives.'' The smoothing comes from the kernel itself: ``using a 2 in the center location provides image smoothing.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/05\_image\_processing/Gonzales,Woods-Digital.Image.Processing.4th.Edition.pdf}, \S10.2 (4th ed., printed pp.~714, 720--722). \textbf{Wording corrected 2026-08-24.} The sentence read ``for which the Sobel filter is \emph{the standard choice}''. The book never says standard; it says \emph{preferable}, and the comparison it draws is against the Prewitt kernels specifically, on the ground of noise suppression. It reserves ``widely used'' for the Canny detector. The sentence now uses the book's own judgement and states the reason it gives, and ``the simpler alternatives'' is the source's own description of Prewitt.}

\citentry*
  {marr1980 --- Marr \& Hildreth (1980)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Yet this operator responds to high-frequency content as strongly as to the boundaries it is meant to mark. \textbf{Marr and Hildreth placed a Gaussian ahead of the derivative operator.} \emph{[Therefore, prefiltering of this kind is the usual precaution against that sensitivity. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{that smoothing precedes differentiation} $\leftarrow$ [\S2:] ``one \textbf{first takes local averages of the image at various resolutions and then detects the changes in intensity} that occur at each one. To realize this idea, we need to determine (a)~\textbf{the nature of the optimal smoothing filter}, and (b)~how to detect intensity changes at a given scale.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that the smoothing filter is the Gaussian} $\leftarrow$ [\S2, The optimal smoothing filter:] ``these two localization requirements, the one in the spatial and the other in the frequency domain, are conflicting\ldots There is, moreover, \textbf{only one distribution that optimizes this relation} (Leipnik 1960), \textbf{namely the Gaussian}.'' [Abstract:] ``the filter for this purpose at a given scale is found to be the \textbf{second derivative of a Gaussian}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/MarrHildreth (2).pdf}, Abstract and \S2 (Proc. R. Soc. Lond. B 207:187--217, printed pp.~190--192). \textbf{Finding, 2026-08-24 --- the mechanism is supported but the stated reason is not.} That Marr and Hildreth put a Gaussian in front of the derivative is beyond doubt. Their \emph{reason} for it is not noise. The paper argues from two premises: that ``the motivation for filtering the image is \textbf{to reduce the range of scales} over which intensity changes take place'', and that the Gaussian uniquely optimises the uncertainty relation between spatial and frequency localisation. The word \emph{noise} appears three times in the whole paper, never as a motivation for the smoothing step. The noise framing carried by ``the usual precaution'' belongs to Gonzalez and Woods, whose \S10.2 opens edge detection with ``1.~Image smoothing for \textbf{noise reduction}'', and to Canny. \textbf{Corrected 2026-08-24.} The sentence previously read ``Marr and Hildreth established Gaussian prefiltering as the usual precaution'', which handed the noise rationale to the one source that does not argue from noise. It is now split. The cited sentence states only what the paper does, which the quotes above support in full. The claim about usual practice moved to the following sentence and carries no citation, since it describes what the field does today rather than anything a 1980 paper asserts.}

\citentry*
  {roshaan2026 --- Roshaan (2026)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Nonetheless, a single gradient operator produces noisy, discontinuous edges even after prefiltering. A preprint by Lino Roshaan addressed this by splitting edge detection into three layers. Depth differences give the silhouette layer. The Scharr and Kirsch gradient operators trace the structure layer. An extended DoG (XDoG) operator recovers the texture layer. Max-pooling merges the three. shows each one alongside the composited result. A luminance-driven gain raises the structure layer's sensitivity in dark regions and holds line quality steady without manual retuning. \textbf{The framework runs as a real-time post-processing shader and needs access to both the colour and the depth buffer.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the three layers and their operators} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.2, Table~1:] Silhouette ($W_1$) $\to$ \textbf{Depth Difference}; Structure ($W_2$) $\to$ \textbf{Scharr, Kirsch}; Texture ($W_3$) $\to$ \textbf{XDoG}. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``Max-pooling merges the three''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.3, Eq.~5:] $\mathrm{Edge}_{\mathrm{final}} = \max\{W_1\cdot\mathrm{Depth},\; W_2\cdot\max(\mathrm{Scharr},\mathrm{Kirsch}),\; W_3\cdot\mathrm{XDoG}\}$. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``A luminance-driven gain\ldots holds line quality steady''} $\leftarrow$ [Contributions:] ``an \textbf{adaptive sensitivity mechanism}\ldots dynamically modulated the edge strength based on \textbf{scene luminance} and depth, \textbf{ensuring consistent line quality}.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``runs as a real-time post-processing shader''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``a unified, \textbf{real-time post-processing shader architecture}''. The depth requirement follows from the silhouette layer, which ``leverages the depth buffer''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Adaptive Hierarchical Edge Detection.pdf}, Abstract, Contributions and \S\S2.1--2.3. \textbf{Citation moved 2026-08-24, the largest placement fix in the thesis.} It previously closed on the second sentence, leaving \emph{six} further Roshaan claims uncited: the three layers, the operator behind each, the max-pooling fusion, the luminance gain, and the buffer requirement. It now closes the block, and every one of those claims is quoted above.}

\citentry*
  {roshaan2026 --- Roshaan (2026)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{The three-layer edge hierarchy of the AHEAD framework: (a) the source frame and (b) the composited result, above the three layers that produce it. (c) The silhouette layer comes from depth differences, (d) the structure layer from gradient operators and (e) the texture layer from the extended DoG operator.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{which operator feeds which layer} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.2, Table~1 ``Hierarchical Classification of Algorithms'':] \textbf{Silhouette ($W_1$) $\to$ Depth Difference}; \textbf{Structure ($W_2$) $\to$ Scharr, Kirsch}; \textbf{Texture ($W_3$) $\to$ XDoG}. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``the silhouette layer comes from depth differences''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.1.4 Depth Difference:] ``This method leverages the \textbf{depth buffer} to isolate object boundaries\ldots \textbf{By detecting discontinuities in depth values, we can specifically target the geometric ``silhouette'' of objects}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``the composited result''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.3, Eq.~5:] $\mathrm{Edge}_{\mathrm{final}} = \max\{W_1\cdot\mathrm{Depth}_{\mathrm{edge}},\; W_2\cdot\max(\mathrm{Scharr}_{\mathrm{edge}},\mathrm{Kirsch}_{\mathrm{edge}}),\; W_3\cdot\mathrm{XDoG}_{\mathrm{edge}}\}$.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Adaptive Hierarchical Edge Detection.pdf}, \S\S2.1.4--2.3 and Table~1. \textbf{REPLACED 2026-08-24:} this caption entry and the prose entry above it previously shared one quote from the abstract, so a single passage was standing in for two different claims. The caption names the operator behind each layer, which the abstract does not do, so it is now sourced to Table~1 and the per-operator sections instead.}

\citentry*
  {kumar2019 --- Pavan Kumar et al. (2019)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} However, neither NPR abstraction nor image filtering is confined to feature detection. Another class of operators, such as the Kuwahara filter, reshapes the texture itself rather than outlining the structures within it. Pavan Kumar et al. grouped this filter with anisotropic and bilateral filtering as NPR filtering techniques. \textbf{It reduces shape, edge and colour detail in a single pass, and the prominent features of the image remain intact.}}
  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the grouping with anisotropic and bilateral filtering} $\leftarrow$ [Introduction:] ``Existence of several \textbf{NPR filtering techniques such as Anisotropic filtering, Kuwahara filter}, Guided filter, Flow-based difference of Gaussian (FDoG) filtering, Structure tensor flow, \textbf{flow-based bilateral filter (FBL)}\ldots'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{what the filter does} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.4, printed p.~150:] ``\textbf{Kuwahara filter simplifies shape, edge, and color simultaneously while preserving important features}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Kumar- A comprehensive survey on NPR.pdf}, Introduction and \S2.4. \textbf{Verbatim lift found and corrected 2026-08-24.} The thesis sentence read ``It simplifies shape, edge and colour at once while preserving important features'', which reproduces the source sentence above with only \emph{simultaneously} changed to \emph{at once} and the British spelling of colour. Quoted material that is not marked as a quotation must be reworded. It now reads ``It reduces shape, edge and colour detail in a single pass, and the prominent features of the image remain intact'', which states the same two claims, the simultaneous action on all three properties and the retention of salient structure, in the thesis's own words. The longest run shared with the source is now three words, \emph{shape, edge and colour}, which are technical nouns with no substitutes.}

\citentry*
  {kuwahara1976 --- Kuwahara et al. (1976)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{It is also described as the classical member of the edge-preserving filters used for image abstraction.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{Primary 1976 chapter not held.} The characterisation is verified against the paper that makes it and that attaches the origin credit to Kuwahara et al.\ in the same sentence. \quad \textbf{(1)} \emph{``the classical member of the edge-preserving filters''} $\leftarrow$ [Kyprianidis et al.\ 2009, \S2 Related Work:] ``Nonlinear filters that \textbf{preserve edges} have been an active research topic for several decades. \textbf{Classical filters in this field are the well-known Kuwahara filter [KHEK76]} and its variants.'' The marker \textbf{[KHEK76]} is Kuwahara, Hachimura, Eiho and Kinoshita (1976). \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``used for image abstraction''} $\leftarrow$ [same paper, Abstract:] ``We present a non-photorealistic rendering technique to transform color images and videos into \textbf{artistic abstractions}. It is based on a generalization of the Kuwahara filter.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_kuwahara.pdf}, \S2 and Abstract. \textbf{Citation decided 2026-08-24, single key.} The sentence previously read ``Kyprianidis et al.\ describe it as\ldots'' with no citation of its own, sitting inside the block closed by the later \texttt{kyprianidis2009} citation. It now states the characterisation directly and cites \texttt{kuwahara1976} alone. \textbf{The reasoning, recorded so it is not re-litigated:} this is the first appearance of the Kuwahara filter as a named technique in Related Work, which \S5.2 of the writing rules says must carry the original paper. The characterisation itself comes from Kyprianidis et al., and a joint citation was considered on that ground, but the survey attributes the filter to Kuwahara et al.\ inside the very sentence quoted above, and \texttt{kyprianidis2009} is cited in its own right two sentences later for the anisotropic generalisation. A second key here would therefore have added no support the reader cannot already reach. \textbf{Still open:} the 1976 Plenum Press chapter is not held, so the origin credit rests on Kyprianidis quoting it rather than on the primary.}

\citentry*
  {kyprianidis2009 --- Kyprianidis et al. (2009)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The anisotropic generalisation of Kyprianidis et al. shapes the sampling region according to local feature geometry. \textbf{The flattening then follows those features instead of cutting across them, and the surface takes on a painted appearance.}}
  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``shapes the sampling region according to local feature geometry''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``It is based on a generalization of the Kuwahara filter that is \textbf{adapted to the local shape of features}, derived from the \textbf{smoothed structure tensor}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``the flattening then follows those features\ldots a painted appearance''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``Contrary to conventional edge-preserving filters, our approach [produces a] \textbf{painting-like flattening effect along the local feature directions} while preserving shape boundaries.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_kuwahara.pdf}, Abstract and \S2. \textbf{Verbatim lift found and corrected 2026-08-24.} The thesis sentence read ``Their anisotropic formulation \emph{adapts the filter to the local shape of features} and this produces a \emph{painting-like flattening effect along the local feature directions}''. Both italicised spans are the abstract word for word, seven words and eight words, inside a single sentence. The sentence was rewritten from scratch: the first claim now reads ``shapes the sampling region according to local feature geometry'' and the second ``the flattening then follows those features instead of cutting across them, and the surface takes on a painted appearance. No run of six or more words survives against the source. The technical content is unchanged.}

\citentry*
  {kyprianidis2011 --- Kyprianidis (2011)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Two artefact problems survived that generalisation, one in noisy regions and one across broad areas of even tone. A later paper by Kyprianidis addressed both. Thresholding the sector weights removes the first, and a multiscale scheme suppresses the second. \textbf{That scheme carries orientation estimates and partial filtering results together through the levels of a smoothed pyramid, and it still runs fast enough for graphics hardware to apply it interactively.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``carries orientation estimates and partial filtering results together through the levels of a smoothed pyramid''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``a \textbf{multi-scale computation scheme} is proposed that \textbf{simultaneously propagates local orientation estimates and filtering results up a low-pass filtered pyramid}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``suppresses the artefacts that appear in broad regions of low contrast''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``This allows for a strong abstraction effect and \textbf{avoids artifacts in large low-contrast regions}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``remains fast enough for graphics hardware to run it interactively''} $\leftarrow$ [\S1:] the scheme is described as one ``that is particularly \textbf{suitable for real-time processing on a GPU}''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_multicale\_209.pdf}, Abstract and \S1. \textbf{Verbatim lift found and corrected 2026-08-24, the largest one in the thesis.} The two sentences read ``\emph{propagates local orientation estimates and filtering results up a low-pass filtered pyramid}'' and ``This \emph{avoids artefacts in large low-contrast regions} and suits \emph{real-time processing on a GPU}. That is a thirteen word run, a six word run and a five word run taken from the abstract and introduction, with only \emph{artifacts} respelled as \emph{artefacts}. All three were rewritten. \textbf{Citation moved in the same pass:} it previously closed on the first sentence, leaving the artefact claim and the GPU claim, both of them Kyprianidis claims, without support. It now closes the block. \textbf{Block restructured 2026-08-24:} the multiscale variant previously arrived with no stated problem to solve, and a sentence about the structure tensor sat in front of it with nothing following from it. The block now opens with the two limitations the paper names, and the credit for each is assigned separately, since the thresholding fixes the noise artefacts and the pyramid fixes the low-contrast ones. Attributing both to the multiscale scheme would have been wrong.}

  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Both formulations derive their kernel shape from a structure tensor, and that computation needs a render pass of its own.} \emph{[compares both variants against the unfiltered input. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{that the 2009 filter derives its kernel from a structure tensor} $\leftarrow$ [Kyprianidis et al.\ 2009, \S3:] ``We begin with \textbf{calculating the structure tensor} and smooth[ing it]\ldots the amount of anisotropy [is] derived from the \textbf{eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the smoothed structure tensor}. Finally, structure-aware smoothing is performed using a nonlinear filter [that] uses weighting functions defined \textbf{over an ellipse, whose shape is based on the local orientation and anisotropy}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that the 2011 variant does the same} $\leftarrow$ [Kyprianidis 2011:] ``in contrast to the other techniques that propagate gradient estimates, in this work \textbf{the structure tensor is propagated}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that the computation is a pass of its own} $\leftarrow$ the tensor is computed from the input image and smoothed before any filtering occurs, so it cannot be produced inside the filtering step it parameterises. Stated as a separate stage in both papers.}

\citentry*
  {kyprianidis2011 --- Kyprianidis (2011)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Painterly abstraction produced by Kuwahara filtering. The first image is the unfiltered input and the second is the single-scale anisotropic filter. \textbf{The third is the multiscale variant.}}
  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the identity of all three panels} $\leftarrow$ [Figure~1 panel labels, printed p.~55:] ``\textbf{(a) Original image}\quad \textbf{(b) Anisotropic Kuwahara filter}\quad \textbf{(c) Proposed method}''. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that panel (c) is the multiscale method and (b) the single-scale one} $\leftarrow$ [Figure~1 caption:] ``Example \textbf{comparing the proposed multi-scale approach with the single-scale approach}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_multicale\_209.pdf}, Figure~1 and its caption, printed p.~55. \textbf{Caption expanded 2026-08-24:} it previously identified only panel~(c), leaving the first two images unexplained. All three are now named, and the panel labels above confirm each one.}

\citentry*
  {wallraven2007 --- Wallraven et al. (2007)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Taken together, this technical catalogue establishes what NPR can render, but not whether the result changes how an avatar is perceived. Psychophysical and VR experiments have examined that question directly. Wallraven et al. compared brush, cartoon and illustrative stylisation on sequences of facial expression. Recognition accuracy held under the cartoon variant but fell under the other two when the sequences were recorded video. \textbf{Computer-generated animations of the same expressions reversed that ordering, and the illustrative variant achieved marginally higher recognition than the cartoon one.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the recorded-video result (Experiment~3)} $\leftarrow$ [\S5 Conclusion:] ``for recognition, for example, we found that \textbf{cartoonized versions were equally well recognized as the original sequences, whereas both brush-stroke and illustrative stylization techniques resulted in a drop in recognition accuracy}, as well as an increase in response times.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the computer-generated result (Experiment~2)} $\leftarrow$ [Article~16/13:] ``here we found that the \textbf{brush-stroke stylization was significantly worse} than the remaining three rendering methods (t test: all $p < 0.05$). In addition, the \textbf{illustrative stylization was slightly better} (t test: $p = 0.05$, $p = 0.07$, marginally significant) \textbf{than both avatar and cartoon conditions}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that the authors themselves flag the split} $\leftarrow$ [\S5:] ``The impact of the stylization methods on the different measures, however, was \textbf{different from Experiment 2}.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{which experiment used which stimuli} $\leftarrow$ [\S4:] ``Experiment 1 + 2 were done with the \textbf{animated sequences}\ldots Experiment 3 was a replication of Experiment 2 with the \textbf{real-world sequences}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/wallraven2007\_stylized\_facial\_expressions.pdf}, \S4, \S5 and the Experiment~2 results. \textbf{Domain error found and corrected 2026-08-25.} The thesis sentence read ``Recognition accuracy held under the cartoon variant but fell under the other two'' with no stimulus type named. That is true of \textbf{Experiment 3 only}, which used recorded video. Experiment~2 ran the same comparison on computer-generated animations and produced a different ordering: the illustrative variant came out marginally \emph{ahead} of the cartoon one. \textbf{This is the experiment that matters here}, since the thesis concerns avatars, so reporting only the video result and leaving it unscoped generalised the wrong half onto the thesis's own domain. \textbf{Both results are now stated.} The paragraph's existing conclusion, that the outcome depends on which technique produces the abstraction rather than on its degree alone, is strengthened rather than weakened by this: the outcome turns out to depend on the stimulus domain as well. \textbf{Hedge preserved:} the Experiment~2 advantage is reported at $p = 0.05$ and $p = 0.07$ and described by the authors as ``marginally significant'', so the thesis says \emph{marginally higher recognition} rather than asserting a clean reversal. Brush-stroke was worst in both experiments and is unaffected.}

\citentry*
  {wisessing2016 --- Wisessing et al. (2016)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Hence, the outcome depends on which technique produces the abstraction, not on its degree alone. Wisessing et al. later demonstrated that toon shading reduces the perceived intensity of facial emotional expression while leaving recognition accuracy intact. \textbf{The authors attribute that effect to the loss of shading information, and this may conceal facial form and features that emotion perception depends on.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the two measured results} $\leftarrow$ [\S6 Discussion, printed p.~28:] ``Our main finding is that the \textbf{shading style used (CG vs.\ toon) does not change the recognition of emotion, but does change the perception of intensity of that emotion}, with toon-shaded renders being rated as displaying emotions as less intense than CG-shaded across the board.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the added mechanism} $\leftarrow$ [same paragraph:] ``It is possible that our toon-shaded animations had lower intensity values than our CG-shaded animations due to the \textbf{lack of visual information}. In particular, the abstraction of the shaded surface \textbf{could obscure facial shape and features crucial to emotion perception}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that the stimulus is facially driven} $\leftarrow$ [\S3 Stimuli Creation:] the character Mery was chosen because she ``has a \textbf{highly controllable facial rig}, with the ability to squash and stretch each part, allowing animators to create highly expressive cartoon animations''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/Wissesing- perception.pdf}. The Discussion column interleaves with the reference list in the extracted text layer, so page~4 was re-read as a rendered image at 190~dpi to confirm the wording. \textbf{A PROPOSED CHANGE WAS WITHDRAWN HERE, 2026-08-25. Recorded so it is not re-proposed.} An audit pass flagged ``facial emotional expression'' as over-specific, on the ground that the abstract calls the rated construct ``the intensity of the \emph{performance}''. Nidanur pushed back, correctly. The paper localises the effect to the face in its own Discussion, quoted above, and the stimulus is a facially rigged character. The flag came from reading the abstract without checking whether the Discussion narrowed the mechanism. It did. Both thesis sentences stand as written, including ``matters most on the face'' in \S3.4. \textbf{Support added instead, same date.} The face mechanism is the strongest sentence in this paper for RQ2 and the thesis was not using it, leaving ``matters most on the face'' as an uncited inference of our own. A second sentence now carries the authors' explanation. \textbf{Hedging matched to the source:} the original is explicitly speculative, ``It is possible that'' and ``could obscure'', so the thesis uses ``attribute'' and ``may conceal'' rather than asserting the mechanism. \textbf{Lift check:} ``obscure facial shape and features crucial to emotion perception'' is seven content words and would have been a verbatim lift. It was reworded to ``conceal facial form and features that emotion perception depends on'', where the longest run shared with the source is two words.}

\citentry*
  {wisessing2020 --- Wisessing et al. (2020)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Characters rendered in this style accordingly convey the correct emotional content without the perceptual amplification that smooth CG shading provides. A follow-up study identified lighting parameters as a further modulator of appeal and eeriness. Higher brightness made characters more appealing regardless of the emotion portrayed. Darker shadows reduced the appeal of cartoon characters and left the appeal of realistic ones unaffected. \textbf{They also raised eeriness, though that effect came mainly from the male character.} \emph{[Together these findings reveal that render style and lighting both shape how a character is received, independently of the emotion being performed. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``Darker shadows raised eeriness''} $\leftarrow$ [\S Realism Experiments, Results:] ``A \textbf{main effect of KTFR on eeriness showed 16:1 was more eerie than 4:1 or 1:1} ($p < 0.0276$ in both cases).'' A higher key-to-fill ratio means less fill light, so 16:1 is the darkest shadow condition. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``reduced the appeal of cartoon characters''} $\leftarrow$ [same section:] ``An interaction between shape and KTFR also occurred\ldots \textbf{For Toon, 1:1 was more appealing than 4:1 or 16:1} ($p < 0.0011$).'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``while leaving the appeal of realistic characters unaffected''} $\leftarrow$ [same sentence:] the interaction ``showed \textbf{no effect of KTFR on appeal ratings for Realistic} (Figure~11).'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{the preceding brightness sentence} $\leftarrow$ [\S Main findings:] ``\textbf{Higher brightness makes for more appealing characters, regardless of the emotion they portray}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/Wissessing-Enloghten me.pdf}, Main findings and the Realism Experiments results. \textbf{Source upgraded 2026-08-24.} The entry previously rested on the bullet-point summary at the front of the paper. It now quotes the statistics behind each of the three claims, taken from the results section itself. \textbf{CORRECTION HISTORY, 2026-08-14:} an earlier version of the thesis sentence claimed brighter light and lighter shadows reduced eeriness for both photorealistic and stylised characters, and that the reduction was larger for photorealistic rendering. That was wrong on both counts and was rewritten. \textbf{Qualification that still applies:} the eeriness main effect above is immediately qualified in the source by ``an interaction between gender, shape, and KTFR, which showed this effect to be \textbf{mainly coming from the male character}. The thesis states the main effect without that qualifier, which is a simplification rather than an error, but it is worth knowing.}

\citentry*
  {zibrek2019tap --- Zibrek et al. (2019)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Those studies measured how a character is read from the outside. Zibrek et al. instead placed observers inside the scene and ran a large between-group VR study with 622 participants. That study compared three render styles from photorealistic to sketch across three emotional scenarios (). The results of that experiment showed that render style left social presence unchanged and produced no eeriness difference between any two individual styles. \textbf{Photorealism shifted which emotions the character evoked rather than strengthening them.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``622 participants\ldots three render styles\ldots three emotional scenarios''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.3:] ``we included responses of \textbf{622 participants} in the final analysis, which was approximately 70 participants (SD: 5.3) for each scenario/render style combination.'' [\S5 Results, opening line:] ``the most robust effects of \textbf{render style (Realistic, Simple, Sketch)} and \textbf{scenario (Sad, Friendly, Unfriendly)}''. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``left social presence\ldots unchanged''} $\leftarrow$ [Table~2, Experiment~1, the Social Presence block reads in its entirety:] ``\textbf{NO EFFECTS}''. Restated in \S8: ``Even though we predicted an effect of render style on Social Presence (H1), we found \textbf{no main effects} or interactions'', and again in the Discussion: ``\textbf{Social presence was not affected by render style}, as we expected.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``and eeriness unchanged''} $\leftarrow$ [Table~2, Affinity block:] the only Eerie row is \textbf{EERIE: Gender} ($\chi^2 = 13.92$, $df = 1$, $p < 0.0002$). No render style row appears for Eerie, so render style produced no effect on it. \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``photorealism shifted which emotions the character evoked rather than strengthening them''} $\leftarrow$ [Table~2, Emotional Response block, where the direction reverses by scenario:] ``CONCERN: Scenario*Render Style ($\chi^2 = 10.35$, $p < 0.04$): \textbf{Sketch style more concern than Realistic} in [one scenario], \textbf{Realistic style more concern than the other two in the Friendly scenario}.'' Also ``EXCITED: Render Style ($\chi^2 = 13.05$, $p < 0.002$): Realistic style rated higher compared to Sketch'' and ``CALM: Scenario*Render Style ($\chi^2 = 12.69$, $p < 0.013$): \textbf{Sketch style more calm than the Realistic}''. [Abstract:] ``our main finding shows that the \textbf{photorealistic character changed the emotional responses} of participants, compared to the stylized versions.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/TAP\_2019.pdf}, Abstract, \S\S4.3--5, Table~2 and the Discussion. \textbf{Source rebuilt 2026-08-24.} The entry previously carried only two short fragments. Every clause of the thesis sentence now has its own quote, and the emotional-response claim is supported by the three table rows whose direction actually reverses between scenarios, which is what makes ``shifted which emotions\ldots rather than strengthening them'' the right description rather than a loose one. \textbf{Scope note:} the paper reports a second, smaller \emph{Additional experiment} (N~=~175, Sad scenario only) whose Table~4 does show \textbf{EERIE: Render style} ($\chi^2 = 7.32$, $p < 0.03$), although its post-hoc found ``no significant differences between individual render styles. The thesis sentence is explicitly about the 622-participant experiment, so it is correct as written, but a reader who opens the paper will meet that second table. \textbf{One further difference worth knowing:} render style \emph{did} affect Place Illusion in Experiment~1 ($\chi^2 = 10.61$, $p < 0.005$, realistic highest). The thesis does not claim otherwise, since it names only social presence and eeriness.}

\citentry*
  {zibrek2019mig --- Zibrek \& McDonnell (2019)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} This challenges the assumption that rendering quality scales linearly with social utility. Zibrek and McDonnell extended this investigation to embodied VR, where participants inhabited a virtual body rather than observing from outside. The results indicated that photorealism significantly raised both self-reported social presence and place illusion, the sense of physically inhabiting the virtual space. \textbf{A rendering change can therefore shift what people report without shifting what they do.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``self-reported social presence''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.3:] ``We found a \textbf{significant difference in Social Presence, where participants felt more present with the Realistic virtual character than the Simple one} ($t(18) = 2.45$, $p = 0.025$, $d = 0.56$).'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``and place illusion''} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.4:] ``($Z = 2.02$, $p = 0.04$, $r = 0.46$), where the \textbf{realistic environment increased the place illusion}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``without shifting what they do''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``This was found to be the case for self-reported social presence and place illusion\ldots \textbf{behavioural measure proximity was not affected by render style}.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``participants inhabited a virtual body''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``\textbf{participants were embodied in a high-fidelity virtual body in VR} and were observing an animated character''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/3359566.3360064.pdf}, Abstract and \S\S2.3--2.4. \textbf{Citation moved 2026-08-24.} It previously closed on the results sentence, leaving the conclusion about self-report diverging from behaviour outside the block. That conclusion reads like an inference but is the paper's own result, quoted above as (3), so it now sits inside the citation.}

  {zibrek2019tap --- Zibrek et al. (2019)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{The three render styles compared by Zibrek et al., from left to right: realistic, simple and sketch, applied to both the character and the room.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``realistic, simple and sketch''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.0.1 Render Styles:] ``We created two styles for our task: a \textbf{mid-range realistic character in ``Simple'' style}, and the \textbf{least realistic ``Sketch'' style} (Figure~2).'' The pair are controls against the realistic baseline: ``In order to estimate the effect of photorealism, we created \textbf{two control render styles}\ldots representing the stylized versions.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``applied to both the character and the room''} $\leftarrow$ [same section:] the two control styles were made ``of \textbf{both the room and the virtual character}''. For Simple, ``we removed the realistic lighting effects, shadows, and other shaders which created a realistic appearance \textbf{of the virtual room and the virtual agent}''. For Sketch, ``removing the \textbf{colour information from the environment} also, leaving only the grayscale range''. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that only the rendering differs} $\leftarrow$ [same section:] ``we \textbf{abstracted the render style only}, while all other elements, such as geometry and animated content, were kept the same.''}

That half is now quoted twice, and the sentence confirming that geometry and animation were held constant is added, since it is what makes this a render-style comparison rather than a whole-stimulus comparison.}

We \textbf{distinguish between stylizations in material and shape by using the prefix m and s respectively}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that the axis runs from a cartoon head to a scanned likeness} $\leftarrow$ [\S3 Stimuli Creation:] the realistic end is a scan, ``To generate the realistic models we replicated the \textbf{multiview-stereo face scanner} of Beeler et al., which reconstructs \textbf{high-resolution textured point clouds} from the photographs of six cameras''. This entry is now sourced to how the \emph{shape} levels were built, from the face scanner at one end to artist-authored cartoon geometry at the other, and to the finding that makes the shape axis the realism axis.}

\citentry*
  {zell2015 --- Zell et al. (2015)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ NPR for Virtual Humans}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Render style is not the only dimension a designer controls. Zell et al. separated a character's shape from its material and varied each independently across five stylisation levels. Shape governed perceived realism, whereas material governed appeal and eeriness. shows the shape axis, which holds the material constant while the geometry moves from a cartoon head at s0 to a scanned likeness at s4. Furthermore, shows the material axis, where the geometry stays fixed and the shader and texture move across the same five levels. Strong mismatches between the two levels also produced very unappealing characters. \textbf{A stylised material on a realistic shape was among those combinations.} \emph{[The uncanny response depends in turn on the level of material stylisation and on its consistency with the shape. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``separated a character's shape from its material and varied each independently across five stylisation levels''} $\leftarrow$ [\S6 Stimuli:] ``For each character, two additional stylizations were created, yielding \textbf{five stylization levels from level 0 (most stylized) to level 4 (highly realistic)}. We \textbf{distinguish between stylizations in material and shape by using the prefix m and s respectively}.'' The independence is procedural: ``we \textbf{transferred all material properties of the baseline characters to the other character shapes}''. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``Shape governed perceived realism, whereas material governed appeal and eeriness''} $\leftarrow$ [Main findings:] ``\textbf{Shape is the key attribute for perceived realism}'' and ``appeal, eeriness, and attractiveness are \textbf{mainly affected by the stylization level of material, and not shape}''. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``the shader and texture move across the same five levels''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3:] ``we denote by \textbf{material the combination of [shader properties] and textures}\ldots For the skin all three characters use the same multi-layer skin shader with subsurface scattering, with \textbf{diffuse albedo specified by a high-resolution texture map}. The \textbf{shader parameters vary between the models} in order to closely resemble the targeted render styles.'' \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``Strong mismatches\ldots produced very unappealing characters. A stylised material on a realistic shape was among those combinations''} $\leftarrow$ [Discussion:] ``\textbf{strong mismatches in the level of stylization of shape and material can result in very unappealing characters}.'' [Results, appeal:] ``We found a \textbf{main effect of material on the ratings of appeal}\ldots but \textbf{no main effect of shape was found}. An interaction between shape and material ($F(4,84)=13.97$, $p<0.0001$) shows that a realistic material on a cartoon shape yields the least appealing combination\ldots and \textbf{the cartoon material on the realistic shape is similarly unappealing} ($p<0.02$).''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/To Stylize or not to Stylize?\_Zell.pdf}, \S3, \S6, Results and Main findings. \textbf{Extended 2026-08-24} when a second sentence was added to the paragraph pointing at the material axis figure alongside the shape axis figure. Quote~(3) is what supports that new sentence: the material dimension is the shader parameters together with the albedo texture, and the transfer procedure in quote~(1) is what keeps it independent of the geometry.}

\citentry*
  {wallraven2007 --- Wallraven et al. (2007)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Research Gap}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Wallraven et al. did compare named techniques for expression recognition, but applied them to the rendered frames as a post-process rather than to the avatar's own shading.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``did compare named techniques''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] ``animated facial expressions are stylized using \textbf{three common techniques (brush, cartoon, and illustrative stylization)}''. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``for expression recognition''} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract:] the comparison uses ``\textbf{task-dependent measures (such as recognizability, intensity)}''. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{``to the avatar's own shading'', the decisive passage} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.3 Stylization for Animated Versus Real-World Sequences:] ``the \textbf{3D geometry of the avatar was lit by an omnidirectional light} coming from slightly above and a \textbf{standard Blinn shader was used to simulate diffuse and specular properties of the skin}.'' The avatar is rendered conventionally. Nothing in the stylisation touches its material. \quad \textbf{(4)} \emph{``applied them to the rendered frames as a post-process''} $\leftarrow$ all three operators take a finished image as input. [\S3.2.1 Brush-Stroke:] ``The \textbf{image stylization process samples the input image} by reading pixel colors at the sampling point positions''. [\S3.2.2 Cartoonlike:] ``\textbf{each input image is processed}\ldots The algorithm\ldots is designed as a \textbf{postprocessing filter in a real-time rendering pipeline}.'' [\S3.2.3 Illustrative:] it ``generates \textbf{output images}, which reproduce the brightness of the \textbf{input image} with black-and-white hatching''. [\S3:] the techniques ``were \textbf{applied to these animated expressions} in order to create stylized sequences''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/Wallraven\_Stylization\_TAP2007.pdf}, Abstract and \S\S3--3.3. \textbf{Reworded 2026-08-24 after a second reading of the primary.} The sentence previously read ``stylised rendered footage \emph{rather than the avatar itself}''. That is true of where the operators act, but it reads as though no avatar was involved, and \S3.1.1 of the paper is titled \emph{The Avatar} and describes a morphable head built from 3D scans driven by motion capture. A reader checking the source would meet that heading and conclude the thesis had it wrong. \textbf{What the second reading added:} \S3.3, quoted above as (3), states outright that the avatar was rendered with an omnidirectional light and a standard Blinn shader. The avatar's own shading is therefore conventional, and the three named techniques run afterwards over the resulting frames. That is exactly the distinction the Research Gap paragraph needs, and the sentence now states it in those terms rather than by implication.}

  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Research Gap}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{That distinction matters most on the face, since Wisessing et al. showed that shading style alters the perceived intensity of an expression.}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {[Results, main effect of shading:] ``A main effect of shading style also occurred ($F(1,22) = 23.2$, $p \approx 0$), with posthoc showing that animations rendered in CG-shading were rated as significantly more intense than those that were toon-shaded ($p < 0.0003$).'' [Recognition accuracy:] the corresponding ANOVA reported a main effect of \emph{emotion} only ($F(4,88) = 5.4$, $p < 0.0007$); no shading-style effect on recognition is reported.}
  {Primary PDF read and verified: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/Wissesing- perception.pdf}. Both halves of the thesis sentence hold: toon-shading lowered rated intensity, while recognition accuracy varied by emotion rather than by shading style.}

This gap is addressed by the iterative technique development and selection described in the following chapters (RQ2). --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {[p.~512, \S2 Obtained Results, final paragraph:] ``The questionnaire also asked to the participants \textbf{which parts of the face} the participant felt more discomfort, if it was the case. Analysing the answers, we could \textbf{highlight two specific parts of the face: eyes and mouth}.''}

\citentry*
  {mcdonnell2012 --- McDonnell et al. (2012)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Research Gap}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} By contrast, whether NPR abstraction on virtual avatars helps or harms perceived trust has been tested directly, and the results disagree. \textbf{McDonnell et al. found appeal and trust ratings lowest in the mid realism range, which stylisation avoids.}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {``[Section 1 Introduction, p. 91:2 (first passage); Section 7 Discussion, p. 91:10 (second and third passages):] ``We found that highly realistic and highly abstract styles were considered appealing, whether still or moving. However, render styles that were in the middle of the range between abstract and realistic were considered unappealing, and the most unappealing character was considered even more so when motion was applied. ... These results are very much in line with the Uncanny Valley theory. Contrary to the theory, however, was the fact that our most realistic character was often rated as appealing or pleasant as our cartoon characters. ... Based on the results of our experiments, we can reassure developers that selecting realistic virtual characters is not as risky as often discussed (provided that the motion does not contain artifacts).''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/ McDonnell, R., Breidt, M., and B\"{u}lthoff, H. H. (2012). Render me Real? Investigating.pdf}. \textbf{Resolved 2026-08-23:} the claim now matches the paper's actual result instead of the one-sided ``principled route out of the uncanny valley''. Verified: ``render styles that were in the middle of the range between abstract and realistic were considered unappealing'', and for trust, ``For the Trustworthy ratings, a main effect of style was also observed (F9,288 = 35.53, p $\approx$ 0)'' with HumanIll ``significantly less trustworthy than all other styles''.}

\citentry*
  {canales2024 --- Canales et al. (2024)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Research Gap}

  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {``[§9 Discussion, p. 8:] ``With regard to Style, we do not see evidence that people are more likely to trust more realistic avatars nor less likely to trust our most stylized caricatures. This runs counter to some previous work that suggests a preference for realistic avatars [18, 25], but is consistent with other work that found no such preference [5, 29]''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Avatar\_Stylization\_and\_Trust\_IEEE\_VR\_2024.pdf}. \textbf{Resolved 2026-08-22:} the plural ``controlled evaluations'' became a single controlled evaluation, matching the one study cited. Canales et al. found no trust penalty for stylisation in their own study, which supports the gist. However the thesis sentence generalises to 'controlled evaluations' in the plural, and this paper explicitly states its result 'runs counter to some previous work that suggests a preference for realistic avatars' (Jo et al. [18], McDonnell et al. [25]). A single-study citation therefore over-generalises; the sentence should either be hedged or carry additional citations.}

\citentry*
  {tao2025 --- Tao et al. (2025)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Research Gap}

  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {``[\S3.6 ``Comparative analysis of trustworthiness'':] ``Eight studies (61.5\%) included comparisons of trustworthiness. The NMA results indicated that the HRA received significantly higher ratings than the LRA (p = 0.046). No significant differences were observed between the MRA group and either the HRA or LRA groups.''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/Tao-Impact.pdf}. Verified 2026-08-23. The thesis sentence claims only the HRA-versus-LRA contrast, which is the one comparison that reached significance. \textbf{Worth knowing:} $p = .046$ is marginal, and the MRA group did not differ significantly from either other group, so this cannot be read as a monotonic trust gain with realism.}

\citentry*
  {canales2024 --- Canales et al. (2024)}
  {Ch.3 Related Work $\rightarrow$ Research Gap}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Thus, no study to date measures both trust and eeriness for an NPR pipeline applied to both the shading and the texture of one photorealistic avatar. \textbf{The Canales study came closest, but it compared coarse style categories and did not treat eeriness as a dimension separate from overall stylisation.} \emph{[Nor has any reviewed study set a real human speaker, a photorealistic avatar of that speaker and an NPR rendering of the same avatar against one another. This gap is addressed by the user study of this thesis (RQ3). --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``compared coarse style categories''} $\leftarrow$ [\S4.1 Avatars:] ``The two stylized versions of each identity were \textbf{hand modeled by a team of artists}\ldots they created concept art for a \textbf{caricatured} version of each person along with a \textbf{semi-realistic} model intended to lie between the caricature and realistic model.'' Three whole-avatar categories, not named operators. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{``did not treat eeriness as a dimension separate from overall stylisation''} $\leftarrow$ this is a claim about what the study \emph{does not} measure, so it is established by inspecting the instrument. Every dependent measure in the paper is a trust prompt on a \textbf{7-point Likert scale}: ``I find this avatar trustworthy'', the ability, benevolence and integrity batteries, and believability. \textbf{No eeriness scale, no uncanniness item and no Ho and MacDorman index appears anywhere in the instrument.} The words \emph{eerie} and \emph{uncanny} occur in the paper only in the related-work review, and afterwards in post-hoc readings of free text, for example ``[participants described] them as uncanny or creepy'' and ``perhaps some uncanniness''. Those are interpretations of comments, not a measured dimension.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Avatar\_Stylization\_and\_Trust\_IEEE\_VR\_2024.pdf}, \S\S4.1--5.3 and \S9. \textbf{Negative claim, verified by exhaustive search 2026-08-24.} A claim that a study did \emph{not} measure something cannot be supported by a quotation, so the whole text was searched for every occurrence of \emph{eerie}, \emph{eeriness} and \emph{uncann}. Twelve occurrences, none of them an instrument item. The thesis sentence is therefore accurate, and it is deliberately narrow: it says eeriness was not treated as a separate dimension, not that the authors ignored the concept.}

\section*{Chapter: Methodology}

\citentry*
  {mori1970 --- Mori (1970)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ Research Questions}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Uncanny valley theory places a real human speaker at the peak of the affinity curve, since maximal human likeness corresponds to maximal comfort.} \emph{[A photorealistic avatar instead sits in the trough immediately before that peak, where negative affect is strongest. That negative affect is what eeriness measures, so how eerie a character feels indicates where on the curve it sits. The question spans both the discomfort a stylised avatar evokes and the trust it receives. If the composite selected under RQ2 relocates the avatar onto the stable rising slope below the trough, it should register as less eerie than the photorealistic avatar that remains in it. The resulting trust level may then approach that of a real human speaker, even though the avatar's human likeness remains lower. RQ3 tests this possibility directly, with the real speaker serving as the reference point against which both avatar renderings are compared. The hypotheses that formalise these comparisons are stated in . --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{that a real human sits at the top of the curve} $\leftarrow$ [\S The significance of the uncanny:] ``In Figure 1, \textbf{a healthy person is at the top of the second peak}. And when we die, we fall into the trough of the uncanny valley.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that maximal human likeness is the endpoint of the axis} $\leftarrow$ [\S The uncanny valley:] ``Of course, \textbf{human beings themselves lie at the final goal of robotics}, which is why we make an effort to build humanlike robots.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that the trough sits immediately before that peak} $\leftarrow$ [same passage:] the descent from the second peak into the valley is the path Mori uses to explain death, ``our impression of death can be explained by the \textbf{movement from the second peak to the uncanny valley}''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/The Uncanny Valley- The Original Essay by Masahiro Mori.pdf} (MacDorman and Kageki translation), \S\S ``The uncanny valley'' and ``The significance of the uncanny. \textbf{Precision note, no change needed.} Mori writes \emph{second} peak, since his curve has an earlier local maximum at the humanoid toy robot. The thesis says ``the peak of the affinity curve. That is correct rather than loose: the second peak is the global maximum of the curve, and the thesis sentence is about where a real human sits, not about how many local maxima the curve has.}

\citentry*
  {mcdonnell2012 --- McDonnell et al. (2012)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} NPR in computer graphics encompasses a wide range of abstraction techniques and these vary in the level of abstraction they produce. \textbf{Neither extreme of that range serves an avatar well, since intermediate realism lowers appeal and heavy abstraction weakens perceived expressive intensity.} \emph{[Selecting an appropriate degree therefore requires calibrating the balance between realism and visual expressiveness. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 6 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``intermediate realism lowers appeal'', stated outright by the authors} $\leftarrow$ [\S Discussion:] ``highly abstract styles were considered appealing, whether still or moving. However, \textbf{render styles that were in the middle of the range between abstract and realistic were considered unappealing}, and the most unappealing character was considered even more so when motion was applied.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the same result from the statistics} $\leftarrow$ [\S6:] ``A main effect of render style was found ($F_{9,288} = 26.54$, $p \approx 0$). Post-hoc analysis showed that \textbf{four of the most abstract and the two most realistic styles were rated as the most appealing} (ToonPencil, ToonFlat, ToonShaded, ToonCG, HumanHQ1, HumanHQ2) and \textbf{significantly more appealing than all other styles} ($p < 0.05$ in all cases).'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{the other half of the thesis sentence is not this paper's} $\leftarrow$ ``heavy abstraction weakens perceived expressive intensity'' is carried by the co-cited \texttt{wallraven2007}, \S5: ``one should, in general, \textbf{avoid high degrees of abstraction} for stylization\ldots as these \textbf{adversely influence the perceived intensity and sincerity}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/McDonnell2012\_RenderMeReal.pdf}, \S6 and Discussion. \textbf{EXCERPT UPGRADED 2026-08-25, prompted by Nidanur questioning whether the sentence was supported.} The entry previously carried only quote~(2), the post-hoc appeal ranking. That reaches ``intermediate realism lowers appeal'' only by inference: the reader has to notice that six of the ten styles sit at the two ends and conclude that the remaining four in the middle are the unappealing ones. Quote~(1) makes the same claim in the authors' own words and was missed on the first pass. The support was always there, but the entry understated it. \textbf{The sentence is a joint citation and each key carries one half.} \texttt{mcdonnell2012} carries the appeal half and measured no expressiveness scale at all, its six scales being realism, appeal, re-assuring, familiar, friendly and trustworthy. \texttt{wallraven2007} carries the expressive-intensity half. Neither key supports the whole sentence alone, and the pairing is therefore necessary rather than decorative. \textbf{Incidental confirmation:} quote~(1)'s phrase ``whether still or moving'' independently corroborates the still-versus-moving correction applied to this key in Related Work on the same day.}

It therefore transfers to avatars without qualification.}
