\citentry*
  {raskar1999 --- Raskar \& Cohen (1999)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V1 — Geometric Silhouette Outline}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Accordingly, a reliable outline generation technique was required as the foundation for all NPR techniques developed in this thesis. The geometric silhouette method was selected since it produces outlines without an edge detection pass. \textbf{It generates an outline shell by enlarging back-facing polygons and relies on GPU face culling to form the outline.} \emph{[The method operates across two dedicated stages: --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the enlargement, in the authors' own term ``fattening''} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.3 View-dependent Modification of Back-facing Polygons:] ``Assuming polygons are convex, \textbf{constant width silhouette edges can be drawn using `fat' back-facing polygons} so that the projection of extra area in the image plane is of constant width. The back-facing polygon is \textbf{fattened by pushing outwards each edge} by a distance proportional to $z/(V \cdot N_B)$ normal to the edge.'' [Figure~4 caption:] ``The backfacing polygon in (a) is \textbf{fattened by pushing the edges outwards} and creating a triangle fan (b). When rendered in black, they appear as silhouettes (c).'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the face culling} $\leftarrow$ [\S3, algorithm listing:] ``\textbf{Enable back face culling}, set depth function to `Less Than'. Render (front facing) polygons in white. \textbf{Enable front face culling}, set depth function `Equal To'. \textbf{Draw (back-facing) polygons in black}.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that it runs per frame with no preprocessing} $\leftarrow$ ``\textbf{To fatten a polygon at run time involves shifting the vertices of the polygon for every new view.}''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Raskar -Image Precision Silhouette Edges.pdf}, \S3 and \S3.3. \textbf{EXCERPT REPLACED 2026-08-25.} This entry had inherited the quote written for the \emph{Related Work} use of the same key, which describes the \textbf{translation} variant, ``translate back-facing polygons towards the camera. The Methodology sentence describes the \textbf{enlargement} variant instead, so the stored excerpt was documenting a different technique from the one the sentence states. The quotes above are the enlargement passages. \textbf{Both variants are Raskar and Cohen's, and they rank them.} The paper rejects translation for this purpose in its own words: ``It is easy to see why \textbf{translation} of back-facing polygons \textbf{will not create uniform line width} silhouettes. The width of the resulting silhouette is dependent on the orientation of the back-facing polygon\ldots'' Enlargement is introduced precisely to fix that. This makes the thesis's split coherent: Related Work presents the simpler translation form to explain the idea, and Methodology adopts the constant-width enlargement form for the implementation. \textbf{Naming corrected the same day.} The technique was called \emph{inverted hull} in 20 places across four chapters. That term appears in \textbf{no article held for this thesis}, not in Raskar and Cohen, not in Isenberg et al., and not in Real-Time Rendering 4th edition. It is a practitioner term from real-time graphics tooling. A reader following either citation would not have found the technique under that name. It is now called the \emph{geometric silhouette outline} throughout, which matches the framing already used in Related Work, ``Silhouette rendering\ldots derives line structure from geometry rather than from the lighting term.}

\citentry*
  {isenberg2003 --- Isenberg et al. (2003)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V1 — Geometric Silhouette Outline}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{This approach is well suited to real-time NPR avatar rendering because it requires no offline mesh preprocessing.} \emph{[It also executes entirely within the GPU vertex transformation stage. As a result, its computational cost scales predictably across assets of varying complexity. However, the expanded shell and the original surface may share similar depth values and this can introduce z-fighting artefacts along the silhouette boundary. A depth bias prevents this by shifting the outline geometry slightly towards the camera in clip space. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 6 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{that preprocessing disqualifies a method for real-time animation} $\leftarrow$ [Table~1, ``Choosing the best silhouette detection algorithm'', journal p.~29:] Requirement ``\textbf{Animate model in real time} beyond a mere flight through the scene'' $\to$ Recommendation ``Real-time technique. \textbf{Object space silhouette detection method that uses preprocessing is not appropriate.}'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{what preprocessing means here} $\leftarrow$ [\S Precomputation methods:] ``To speed up the process of silhouette edge determination, various authors have developed methods that accomplish some type of \textbf{preprocessing}. The preprocessing stage sets up data structures used to find silhouette edges more quickly.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Isenberg\_2003\_ADG.pdf}, Table~1 and \S Precomputation methods. \textbf{Correctly attributed, no change.} This is the survey's own recommendation table rather than a restatement of another paper, so \texttt{isenberg2003} is right here even though the neighbouring technique sentence is not. \textbf{Worth knowing:} Raskar and Cohen make the same claim about their own method in their abstract, ``The method does not require any preprocessing or adjacency information and hence is applicable for dynamic scenes'', so either key would be defensible. The survey states it as a general design rule, which is what the thesis sentence does, so it stays.}

\citentry*
  {isenberg2003 --- Isenberg et al. (2003)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V1 — Geometric Silhouette Outline}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Despite its stability and low rendering cost, the geometric silhouette method has several limitations when applied to avatar rendering. shows the results across all three platforms. Surfaces with degenerate or coincident normals, such as flat planes, yield no visible outline because displacing vertices along identical surface normals does not generate an expanded shell. In addition, the method does not register interior surface boundaries. Consequently, creases between body regions, clothing seams and facial contours receive no edge marking. \textbf{The expanded shell yields only the outer contour, which in the terminology of Isenberg et al. excludes the internal silhouettes lying inside it.} \emph{[This limitation is shared by geometry-based outline methods and motivates the interior edge detection techniques introduced in V3. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 7 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the contour versus internal silhouette distinction} $\leftarrow$ [\S Definitions and terminology, journal p.~28:] ``We define a \textbf{contour as the subset of the silhouette that separates the object from the background}. Also, the subset of the silhouette, which in 2D divides one portion of the object from another one of the same object, is not part of the contour. Those lines are sometimes called \textbf{internal silhouettes}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Isenberg\_2003\_ADG.pdf}, p.~28. \textbf{Citation placement checked and correct.} The three sentences before this one report limitations observed in this project's own implementation and reference this thesis's own figure. They are uncited, which \S5.1 rule 5 requires. The citation attaches only to the sentence that uses the survey's terminology.}

\citentry*
  {lake2000 --- Lake et al. (2000)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V2 — Toon Shading}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Toon shading achieves stylisation by simplifying the tonal representation of a surface rather than reproducing physically accurate illumination. Discretising the illumination response into uniform tonal regions produces the characteristic flat appearance associated with hand-drawn animation. \textbf{Correspondingly, the approach is well suited to interactive character rendering.} \emph{[Toon shading was therefore selected as the V2 baseline because it provides a controlled starting point from which each subsequent technique introduces a targeted increase in stylisation. However, implementing this stylisation across the three avatar platforms requires adaptations that account for differences in each platform's rendering architecture. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{that the technique is a real-time one} $\leftarrow$ [Abstract, Results:] ``In this paper we introduce \textbf{new real-time algorithms for cartoon shading}, pencil sketching, and silhouette edge detection and rendering.'' The paper's own title is \emph{Stylized Rendering Techniques For Scalable \textbf{Real-Time} 3D Animation}. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that the tonal range runs from flat to smooth} $\leftarrow$ [\S3:] ``single-color flat shading (one texel value) at one end of the spectrum, and \textbf{smooth diffuse shading at the other}. By providing the user with just a few parameters, our Painter offers a large variety of stylized and cartoon rendering effects.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/lake et al Stylized rendering.pdf}, Abstract and \S3. \textbf{Correctly attributed.} Both halves are the authors' own contribution rather than restated prior work.}

The resulting tonal structure consists of highlight, midtone, shadow and deep shadow regions. A higher abstraction level reduces local tonal contrast and softens transitions between shading regions towards a flatter, more uniform appearance. illustrates the ramp texture used in this work. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}

\citentry*
  {barla2006 --- Barla et al. (2006)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V2.2 — X-Toon Shading}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Barla et al. further described a general attribute mapping mechanism that maps a chosen surface attribute onto the abstraction axis and they demonstrated it with depth-based and orientation-based maps.} \emph{[Three driving modes were evaluated here: depth, curvature and manual control. Depth mode and manual mode coincide on the Meta Avatars SDK, since the avatar is framed head and shoulders and its visible surface spans too narrow a depth range for the depth-driven abstraction value to vary. Curvature mode stays distinct there, because it responds to local surface orientation rather than to distance from the camera. The user study used a fixed camera distance much closer to the avatar than the general platform comparison setup. At that distance the depth and curvature modes produce almost no variation, as explains in detail. X-Toon produced a softer and more controllable stylised appearance than V2, since the stylisation level can be adjusted independently of the illumination response. It was consequently retained as the toon shading component in all later technique combinations. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}

  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Silhouette extraction techniques can be classified by how they detect edges, into image space, object space and hybrid approaches.} \emph{[Unlike the object-space outline method introduced in V1, image-space techniques analyse per-pixel information generated during rendering to identify visually significant boundaries. One commonly used cue is the surface normal, whose orientation changes abruptly at creases, folds and other geometric features. \citentry*
  {roshaan2026 --- Roshaan (2026)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V5 — Hierarchical Edge Detection with Multiple Cues}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Hierarchical edge detection combines multiple complementary boundary cues into a single, unified representation rather than relying on any one signal alone.}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the three layers and their operators} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.2, Table~1 ``Hierarchical Classification of Algorithms'':] \textbf{Silhouette ($W_1$) $\to$ Depth Difference}; \textbf{Structure ($W_2$) $\to$ Scharr, Kirsch}; \textbf{Texture ($W_3$) $\to$ XDoG}. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the fusion into one representation} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.3, Eq.~4:] $\mathrm{Edge}_{\mathrm{final}} = \max\{W_1 \cdot \mathrm{Silhouette},\, W_2 \cdot \mathrm{Structure},\, W_3 \cdot \mathrm{Texture}\}$, introduced as ``To combine the results of all the implemented algorithms, we implement the method of \textbf{max-pooling}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Adaptive Hierarchical Edge Detection.pdf}, \S\S2.2--2.3 and Table~1. \textbf{Correctly attributed.}}

\citentry*
  {roshaan2026 --- Roshaan (2026)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V5 — Hierarchical Edge Detection with Multiple Cues}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} V5 adopts this approach as a post-processing architecture that operates on the rendered image rather than on the source texture. Depth discontinuities recover object silhouettes and surface normal discontinuities reveal geometric structure. Neither cue reads surface colour, and this reduces the dependence on albedo quality identified in V4.2. Colour boundaries that leave no geometric trace require a third cue, and the colour layer uses the Roberts Cross operator for it. \textbf{The original formulation derives its structure layer from gradient operators instead of from surface normals.}}
  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {\textbf{This citation supports a CONTRAST, not a borrowing. It is attached to the one sentence in the block that describes what AHEAD does differently from V5.} \quad \textbf{(1)} \emph{that AHEAD's structure layer uses gradient operators} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.2, Table~1 ``Hierarchical Classification of Algorithms'':] \textbf{Structure ($W_2$) $\to$ Scharr, Kirsch}. The other two rows are Silhouette ($W_1$) $\to$ Depth Difference and Texture ($W_3$) $\to$ XDoG. \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that AHEAD has no normal layer at all, which is the stronger form of the claim} $\leftarrow$ [Figure~4, pipeline architecture:] the framework's only inputs are a \textbf{Color Buffer} and a \textbf{Depth Buffer}, feeding an Adaptive Scalar (Luminance Gain), a Structure Layer (Kirsch/Scharr), a Texture Layer (XDoG) and a Silhouette Layer (Depth Difference), combined by Max-Pooling Fusion. \textbf{No normal buffer is read anywhere in the pipeline}, so a normal-based layer is not merely absent from Table~1, it is unavailable to the architecture as published.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Adaptive Hierarchical Edge Detection.pdf}, \S2.2 Table~1 and Figure~4. \textbf{Excerpt written 2026-08-25 after Nidanur queried how this entry was supported.} The entry had no source excerpt at all: an earlier override for this key matched the preceding entry and left this one bare, so the checker displayed the whole paragraph as the covered block with nothing to check it against. That is why it read as unsupported. \textbf{Citation placement checked and correct.} Five of the six sentences in this block describe this thesis's own design and carry no citation, which is what \S5.1 rule~5 requires: the adoption of a post-process architecture, the depth and normal layers of V5, the choice of Roberts Cross for the colour layer, the substitution of the normal cue, and the per-layer weighting. \texttt{roshaan2026} attaches only to the sentence reporting AHEAD's own structure layer. \textbf{Why the contrast matters, and why it is worth stating.} V5 is not a reimplementation of AHEAD. It keeps the three-layer hierarchy and the max-pooling fusion but replaces the structure cue, and the following sentence gives the reason in the thesis's own voice: baked illumination in the source texture would otherwise register as geometric detail. Without this sentence a reader comparing V5 against the cited paper would find a normal layer that the paper does not have and could take it for a misreading of the source rather than a deliberate departure. \textbf{CORRECTION 2026-08-26.} An earlier draft of this entry implied that the Roberts Cross operator does not appear in AHEAD. \textbf{It does.} \S2.1.5 is a dedicated subsection on it, citing Roberts~(1963) as its reference~[14], and Figure~1(c) prints the two kernels under the caption ``Edge detection kernels used in the Structure Layer. What is accurate is that AHEAD \emph{catalogues and then rejects} it: \S2.1.5 warns that ``it is very sensitive to fine texture details. It picks up too many details cluttering the screen\ldots too much detail might create a harsh look, and \S2.2 Selection of Algorithms explains the choice of Scharr and Kirsch instead, which is what Table~1 records. The thesis therefore adopts, for its colour layer, an operator the source considered and set aside for a different application. That is not a contradiction of the source and needs no separate citation, but the distinction should not be misstated: the entry above is correct that AHEAD's \emph{structure layer} uses gradient operators rather than surface normals, and that is the only claim the thesis sentence makes.}

\citentry
  {roshaan2026 --- Roshaan (2026)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V5 — Hierarchical Edge Detection with Multiple Cues}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} V5 substitutes the normal cue there, because baked illumination in the source texture would otherwise register as geometric detail. Each layer carries an independent weight and the responses are blended through adjustable parameters so that the contribution of each cue can be controlled interactively. \textbf{Since a gradient operator produces weak responses in dark regions that fall below the detection threshold, a further mechanism from the same framework scales the edge response inversely with local luminance.} \emph{[V5 applies this gain on the Meta Avatars SDK, where the composited output is darker than the raw albedo the other two platforms read. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{the problem the gain solves} $\leftarrow$ [\S2.4 Adaptive Feature Enhancement:] ``in darker regions of the game, the \textbf{standard gradient operator produces a weak gradient response that frequently drops below the binary threshold}.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that the scalar is inverse in luminance} $\leftarrow$ [same section, Eq.~6:] ``The \textbf{adaptive scalar $S(x,y)$ is modeled as a modified inverse-linear function of the local pixel luminance} $L(x,y)$'', with $S(x,y) = \min\!\left(\tfrac{1}{k \cdot L(x,y) + \epsilon},\, S_{max}\right)$, $k = 3.0$, $\epsilon = 0.1$, $S_{max} = 4.0$. The section closes ``\textbf{Lower luminance areas have hi[gher gain]}''. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that it is a distinct stage of the same framework} $\leftarrow$ [Figure~4, pipeline:] the \textbf{Adaptive Scalar (Luminance Gain)} block sits alongside the Structure, Texture and Silhouette layers.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Adaptive Hierarchical Edge Detection.pdf}, \S2.4 and Figure~4. \textbf{New citation added 2026-08-26, closing a chapter-ownership gap.} The adaptive gain was credited in the Implementation chapter but never introduced in Methodology, so a mechanism taken from a cited paper appeared only in a chapter of code detail. \S6.1 assigns the conceptual description to Methodology. The gap is the same shape as the one found for \texttt{blinn1977} on 2026-08-25, but the opposite resolution applies: Blinn-Phong is standard shading vocabulary and its citation was dropped, whereas this gain is genuinely AHEAD's contribution and needed introducing rather than removing. \textbf{\S5.1 rule 5 split in place.} The cited sentence states only what the framework does. The sentence after it, ``V5 applies this gain on the Meta Avatars SDK, where the composited output is darker than the raw albedo the other two platforms read'', describes this project's own use and carries no citation. \textbf{Origin check (\S5.3a), and a deliberate omission.} Roshaan justifies the gain perceptually by the \textbf{Weber-Fechner Law}, credited inline to reference~[6], and separately credits the AGC framing to ``photographic tone reproduction [13]. Neither source is held. The thesis therefore uses Roshaan's own engineering observation instead, that weak gradients in dark regions fall below the detection threshold, which requires no second key. If the perceptual justification is ever wanted in the text, the Weber-Fechner reference must be obtained first.}

\citentry
  {winnemoller2006}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Design $\rightarrow$ V5 — Hierarchical Edge Detection with Multiple Cues}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} compares the results across the three avatar platforms. V5 detects a broader range of structural boundaries on the Mixamo and Avaturn rendered frames. The depth threshold has little visual influence, since the geometric silhouette outline already provides the silhouette. The normal and colour thresholds instead control the density of interior contours. These contours trace geometric curvature, appearing along the underside of the nose and the outer lip edges, but do not produce clear boundaries between adjacent facial segments. Furthermore, the colour gradient layer occasionally responds to illumination gradients in the rendered image, particularly around the nose bridge where shading transitions are interpreted as texture edges. Although combining the remaining cues still produces richer interior detail than the previous techniques, the absence of screen-space depth prevents silhouette detection on the Meta Avatars SDK. The composited SDK output contains baked lighting gradients absent from the raw albedo, making the texture layer more prone to false responses on this platform. \textbf{The colour layer therefore replaces the Roberts Cross computation with the XDoG operator of Winnem\"{o}ller et al. on this platform.} \emph{[This reduced these false responses, as (b) shows. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{$\tau$ and $\phi_e$, the parameters the thesis shader implements} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.2:] ``\textbf{The parameter $\tau$ in Equation 4 controls the amount of center-surround difference required for cell activation, and $\phi_e$ controls the sharpness of the activation falloff.}'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the operator itself} $\leftarrow$ [Eq.~4:] $D = 1$ if $(S_{\sigma_e} - \tau \cdot S_{\sigma_r}) > 0$, otherwise \textbf{$1 + \tanh(\phi_e \cdot (S_{\sigma_e} - \tau \cdot S_{\sigma_r}))$}.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/videoabstraction.pdf}, \S3.2 and Eq.~4. \textbf{Methodology site of the same correction.} See the Implementation entry for this key for the full account. In short: the operator is the 2006 paper's, \emph{XDoG} is the 2012 paper's name for it, and the thesis shader's \texttt{\_XDoGTau} and \texttt{\_XDoGPhi} are $\tau$ and $\phi_e$ from Equation~4 above. Both keys are cited.}

\citentry
  {isenberg2003 --- Isenberg et al. (2003)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ Avaturn Avatar Preprocessing}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The head albedo texture was preprocessed in Blender in order to reduce the edge detection artefacts caused by the reconstructed albedo texture identified in . The avatar was then re-exported as a GLB file. Four texture operations were applied to address two distinct problems in the head albedo: baked illumination variation that introduced spurious edge responses at boundaries unrelated to surface structure and the absence of a strong tonal cue at the lip boundary. \textbf{The first of these is the characteristic liability of colour-buffer edge detection.}}
  {Use 9 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``the characteristic liability of colour-buffer edge detection''} $\leftarrow$ [\S Image space algorithms, journal p.~29:] ``The easiest way to find significant lines would be to \textbf{detect edges in the color buffer}. This, however, \textbf{doesn't necessarily detect silhouettes since changes in shading and texturing can cause erroneous edge detection}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/Isenberg\_2003\_ADG.pdf}, journal p.~29. \textbf{Added 2026-08-24} so that the preprocessing section names the problem it solves as the one the survey literature already identifies. The corresponding Related Work sentence is the entry for this same passage in \S3.3. This is a pointer rather than a re-explanation: the mechanism is not restated here.}

\citentry
  {demsar2006 --- Dem\v{s}ar (2006)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ Parameter Adjustment Study}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Participants were required to assign a unique rank to every image in the round. Any ballot with a missing rank or a tied rank was excluded. Images were converted to Borda-count points in each round, where the image ranked last received 1 point and the image ranked first received points equal to the number of options. The totals were then summed across participants. \textbf{An omnibus Friedman test established whether the ranking within a round differed from chance and a pairwise Wilcoxon signed-rank test compared the configuration ranked first against its closest competitor.} \emph{[The same nonparametric approach is applied to the advisor ranking task in the main study (). reports the outcome of each round and the reasoning that fixed each value. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {[Abstract / Recommendation:] ``We recommend Friedman test, with the corresponding post-hoc tests for comparison of more classifiers over multiple data sets.'' [Sec.~4.1:] ``For a two classifier comparison, the Wilcoxon signed ranks test is more appropriate \ldots we use average ranks \ldots Friedman test \ldots followed by post-hoc tests.'' [Sec.~3.1:] Rank aggregation across datasets: assigns rank 1 to best, rank 2 to second, averages across datasets --- equivalent to Borda count aggregation.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/demsar06a.pdf}. Confirm this passage supports the sentence above.}

Dubosc et al.\ is a within-subjects VR study (n~=~40) rendering the \emph{same} actress-driven avatar at three stylisation levels with animation, voice and identity held constant, which mirrors the C2-versus-C3 isolation. \textbf{Caveats:} the stylised-versus-realistic comparison sits exactly at $p = .05$ and the effect is small ($\eta^2 = .045$); the trustworthiness advantage is reported only as a trend ($p = .075$), so trust must not be described as a significant gain from this source.}

\citentry
  {ho2010 --- Ho \& MacDorman (2010)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Objective and Hypotheses}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Eeriness is a distinct negative affective response rather than general dislike.}}
  {Use 4 of this source.}
  {``[Section 1 Plotting emotional responses to humanlike characters, p. 1508:] ``Mori (1970) refers to negative shinwakan as bukimi, which translates as eeriness. However, eeriness is not the negative anchor of warmth. A person can be cold and disagreeable without being eerie--at least not eerie in the way that an artificial human being is eerie. In addition, the set of negative emotions that predict eeriness (e.g., fear, anxiety, and disgust) are more specific than coldness (Ho, MacDorman, \& Pramono, 2008). Thus, shinwakan and bukimi appear to constitute distinct dimensions.''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Ho2010UncannyValleyIndices.pdf}. Verified against the primary source. The passage states directly that eeriness is not simply the negative pole of warmth or likeability and is predicted by a more specific set of negative emotions. Reinforced in Section 3, p. 1512: 'Eeriness, which is conceptually distinct from negative warmth (i.e., interpersonal coldness), would need to be included in any set of indices on the uncanny valley.'}

\citentry
  {mori1970 --- Mori (1970)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Objective and Hypotheses}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{Uncanny valley theory places a real human speaker at the peak of the affinity curve, where human likeness is maximal and comfort is highest.}}
  {Use 6 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{that a real human sits at the top of the curve} $\leftarrow$ [\S The significance of the uncanny:] ``In Figure 1, \textbf{a healthy person is at the top of the second peak}. And when we die, we fall into the trough of the uncanny valley.'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{that maximal human likeness is the endpoint of the axis} $\leftarrow$ [\S The uncanny valley:] ``Of course, \textbf{human beings themselves lie at the final goal of robotics}, which is why we make an effort to build humanlike robots.'' \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{that the trough sits immediately before that peak} $\leftarrow$ [same passage:] the descent from the second peak into the valley is the path Mori uses to explain death, ``our impression of death can be explained by the \textbf{movement from the second peak to the uncanny valley}''.}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/The Uncanny Valley- The Original Essay by Masahiro Mori.pdf} (MacDorman and Kageki translation), \S\S ``The uncanny valley'' and ``The significance of the uncanny. \textbf{Precision note, no change needed.} Mori writes \emph{second} peak, since his curve has an earlier local maximum at the humanoid toy robot. The thesis says ``the peak of the affinity curve. That is correct rather than loose: the second peak is the global maximum of the curve, and the thesis sentence is about where a real human sits, not about how many local maxima the curve has.}

\citentry
  {alipour2025 --- Alipour et al. (2025)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Procedure and Measures}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Parts E through G record trusting beliefs, that is, what a participant holds to be true of the advisor. P1 instead records a trusting intention, a willingness to depend on that advisor. \textbf{The two need not agree, and measuring both addresses the methodological gap identified by Alipour et al..} \emph{[The trustworthiness and unsettlingness rankings provided behavioural analogues to the corresponding Likert measures, Parts E through G and Part D respectively. Ranked and rated judgements can therefore be compared directly for both dimensions. A rank order was used for all three questions rather than a forced binary choice, since both avatar conditions were expected to score below C1. Limiting participants to two options would concentrate responses on C1 and leave insufficient variance to distinguish C2 from C3. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {``[Section 5.2 Methodological limitations of the existing literature, p. 16; and Section 5.3 Research gaps and recommendations for future research, p. 17 (also Table 4, gap: 'Overreliance on self-report measures', recommendation: 'Combine subjective surveys with objective behavioral outcomes'):] ``Finally, almost all studies relied heavily on self-report measures, which are vulnerable to biases such as social desirability and participants' misinterpretation of constructs. Moreover, self-report measures may fail to capture automatic or unconscious reactions, which are especially relevant in HAI research. For example, participants may consciously report trust while unconsciously exhibiting hesitation or discomfort. These points highlight the need for integrating behavioral or physiological measures in future research to more reliably capture UVE-related responses. ... We also recommend complementing single-time subjective self-report measures with additional methodological approaches, such as implicit trust tasks, decision-making scenarios or trust games, behavioral indicators (e.g., advice following, task delegation), and physiological signals''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/alipour Would you rely on an eerie agent?.pdf}. Verified against the primary source. The paper explicitly names overreliance on self-report as a methodological gap, states that self-report may miss automatic or unconscious reactions, and recommends implicit trust tasks, decision-making scenarios and behavioural indicators such as advice following. A forced-choice advisor selection is the kind of measure the review counts as objective: it classifies a choice-task decision preference among its seven objective trust measures (Section 4.3.2, p. 9).}

\citentry
  {demsar2006 --- Dem\v{s}ar (2006)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Procedure and Measures}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The primary analysis was a one-way repeated-measures analysis of variance (ANOVA) per subscale composite (B\_score, D\_score, E\_score, F\_score, G\_score), with condition (C1, C2, C3) as the within-subjects factor. Every hypothesis concerns an individual subscale rather than a linear combination of them and the subscales were accordingly analysed separately and not through a multivariate omnibus test. Pairwise comparisons between the three conditions followed, corrected by Holm's step-down procedure. \textbf{Holm's procedure is more powerful than the Bonferroni correction and makes no additional assumptions about the hypotheses tested.}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {See entry \#115 for the source passage.}
  {Already checked above. Confirm the same passage also supports \emph{this} sentence.}

  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Procedure and Measures}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Effect sizes were reported as partial $\eta^2$. \textbf{Comparable rating studies of avatar appearance have analysed within-participant conditions with the repeated-measures ANOVA.}}
  {Use 5 of this source.}
  {\textbf{[Results, appeal:]} ``We found a \textbf{main effect of material on the ratings of appeal}\dots\ \textbf{but no main effect of shape was found}. An interaction between shape and material ($F(4,84)=13.97$, $p<0.0001$) shows that a realistic material on a cartoon shape yields the least appealing combination\dots\ and \textbf{the cartoon material on the realistic shape is similarly unappealing} ($p<0.02$).'' \quad \textbf{[Results, eeriness:]} the scale is ``Extremely eerie---Extremely re-assuring''; ``we found a \textbf{main effect of material on the ratings of reassurance}\dots\ \textbf{but no main effect was found for shape}.'' \quad \textbf{[Realism:]} ``\textbf{shape is the dominant factor when rating realism} and expression intensity, while \textbf{material is the key component for appeal}.'' \quad \textbf{[Conclusion, the mismatch framing in the authors' own words:]} ``\textbf{Matching levels of stylization of geometry and material cause the highest ratings of appeal, while strong mismatches (e.g., very realistic material on a stylized shape) result in unappealing and eerie characters}.''}
  {Read from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/To Stylize or not to Stylize?\_Zell.pdf} (13 pages). \textbf{Verified 2026-08-22.} All three thesis claims hold. The mismatch framing is the authors' own conclusion, not an inference. Two further findings in the same passage are not yet used in the thesis and would strengthen Evaluation \S6.3: ``the effect of material stylizations grows when shape realism is increased\dots\ mismatches \dots\ are less prominent on abstract characters'' (C3 has a photogrammetric shape, so material mismatch bites hardest there), and ``subtle stylization of a realistic material (edge-preserving blur on the albedo texture) increases appeal without sacrificing realism'' (essentially the head-texture preprocessing step).}

\citentry
  {mcdonnell2012 --- McDonnell et al. (2012)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Procedure and Measures}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Effect sizes were reported as partial $\eta^2$. \textbf{Comparable rating studies of avatar appearance have analysed within-participant conditions with the repeated-measures ANOVA.}}
  {Use 7 of this source.}
  {``[Section 5.1.3 Results, p. 91:5 (first passage); Section 5.1 Exp 1: Still vs. Moving, p. 91:4 (second passage):] ``For each of the rating scales, a two-way repeated measures ANalysis Of VAriance (ANOVA) was conducted on the data from both blocks with within-subjects factor render style (10) and between-subjects factor movement (2 - still or moving). ... In psychology, rating scales are commonly used to gather information on subjective conditions such as attitudes and emotions of participants, which could not be collected directly.''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/ McDonnell, R., Breidt, M., and Bülthoff, H. H. (2012). Render me Real? Investigating.pdf}. \textbf{Resolved 2026-08-22:} the sentence now reports practice (``Comparable rating studies\ldots have analysed'') instead of asserting an established test, and says ``avatar appearance'' so this citation fits. The paper demonstrates the practice, running two-way repeated-measures ANOVAs with render style as the within-subjects factor across all six rating scales and again in Exp 2 and Section 6.2. It nowhere claims that repeated-measures ANOVA is 'the established test' for such studies, and it makes no methodological survey statement of that kind. The paper is only a single instance of the practice, so it supports the sentence by example rather than by assertion. Note also that the paper's own Exp 1 factor 'movement' was between-groups, not within-participants.}

The paper demonstrates the practice (a within-subjects avatar-appearance experiment analysed with two-factorial repeated-measures ANOVAs) but never makes the normative methodological claim that repeated-measures ANOVA 'is the established test'; I searched the full text for ANOVA, repeated, within-subject, design and found only this reported use. The manipulation is also avatar model realism (wooden mannequin vs photogrammetry scan), not rendering style, so it is an instance rather than a warrant for the thesis sentence.}

  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Procedure and Measures}

The Greenhouse-Geisser correction was applied where sphericity was violated. \textbf{Both instruments report Cronbach's $\alpha$ per subscale, with .70 as the accepted reliability threshold.} \emph{[The same was done here before the composites were formed. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 6 of this source.}
  {``[Section 2.1.3 Statistical analysis, p. 1510; Section 3.1 Research goal, p. 1513:] ``Cronbach's $\alpha$ was used to measure the reliability of each index. ... [Section 3.1:] (4) there should be at least three semantic differential scales per index to enable the estimation of reliability; and (5) the reliability of the indices should be acceptable (Cronbach's $\alpha$ $\geq$ .70).''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/02\_trust\_and\_avatar\_perception/Ho2010UncannyValleyIndices.pdf}. \textbf{Resolved 2026-08-22:} the sentence was split so the citation covers only what the two instruments do (report $\alpha$ per subscale against a .70 threshold); this project's own procedure now stands uncited. The paper supports the practice: it reports Cronbach's $\alpha$ separately for every index (Tables 1, 3, 4, 5) and applies the .70 cutoff, e.g. 'perceived safety had low reliability with a Cronbach's $\alpha$ of .60, which is below the standard .70 cutoff (Nunnally, 1978)' (Section 2.2, p. 1511). It does not, however, state the rationale the thesis sentence gives, that averaging items presumes they measure one construct; unidimensionality is handled in the paper by factor analysis, not by Cronbach's $\alpha$ ('items corresponding to a single, unidimensional concept should load on the same factor when applying exploratory factor analysis', Section 3.1, p. 1513). Note also that the thesis sentence describes its own analysis procedure rather than a claim from this paper. The $\alpha$ and $\geq$ glyphs are transcribed from the PDF's font-substituted extraction ('a' and 'P').}

\citentry
  {lakens2017 --- Lakens (2017)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Procedure and Measures}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The theoretically critical pairwise comparison is C2 versus C3. H2 predicts equivalence rather than a difference and this leads to a non-significant pairwise result providing insufficient evidence on its own. \textbf{A TOST (two one-sided tests) equivalence test was therefore applied to the C2 versus C3 trust composite.}}
  {First use of this source.}
  {``[Abstract:] ``Scientists should be able to provide support for the absence of a meaningful effect. Currently, researchers often incorrectly conclude an effect is absent based a nonsignificant result. A widely recommended approach within a frequentist framework is to test for equivalence. In equivalence tests, such as the two one-sided tests (TOST) procedure discussed in this article, an upper and lower equivalence bound is specified based on the smallest effect size of interest.'' [p.~356:] ``A very simple equivalence testing approach is the `two one-sided tests' (TOST) procedure (Schuirmann, 1987). In the TOST procedure, an upper ($\Delta_U$) and lower ($\Delta_L$) equivalence bound is specified based on the smallest effect size of interest (SESOI; e.g., a positive or negative difference of $d =.3$).''''}
  {Open \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/Lakens -equivalence Test.pdf}, abstract and p.~356, left column.}

\citentry
  {demsar2006 --- Dem\v{s}ar (2006)}
  {Ch.4 Methodology $\rightarrow$ User Study Design $\rightarrow$ Procedure and Measures}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Three post-study ranking questions, covering P1, trustworthiness and unsettlingness, were each analysed with a Friedman test comparing mean advisor ranks across conditions. \textbf{Pairwise Wilcoxon signed-rank tests were then applied to each pair, with Holm correction to control the family-wise error across the comparisons.} \emph{[C2 versus C3 is the critical pairwise comparison in each case. Rank responses carry only ordinal information. The parametric assumptions above thus do not apply. The parameter adjustment study used the same procedure (), meaning both rest on one statistical basis. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {``[Section 3.2.2 Friedman Test, printed page 13:] ``Although we here use these procedures only as post-hoc tests for the Friedman test, they can be used generally for controlling the family-wise error when multiple hypotheses of possibly various types are tested.''''}

Demsar supports applying Holm generally to control family-wise error over multiple hypotheses of various types, but he never pairs Holm with pairwise Wilcoxon signed-rank tests: his post-hoc procedures operate on average-rank z statistics (Nemenyi, Bonferroni-Dunn, Holm, Hochberg, Hommel), and he cautions against all-pairs testing ('We thus should not make pairwise comparisons when we in fact only test whether a newly proposed method is better than the existing ones', p. 12). The 'C2 versus C3 is the critical pairwise comparison' clause is the thesis's own study content and is not addressed by this source.}

\section*{Chapter: Implementation}

\citentry
  {roshaan2026 --- Roshaan (2026)}
  {Ch.5 Implementation $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Implementation $\rightarrow$ V5 — Hierarchical Edge Detection}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} This proxy detects distance discontinuities within the avatar mesh. However, it cannot separate the avatar from the background. The background lies entirely outside the per-fragment computation. \textbf{The normal layer reuses the world-space normal derivative computation from V3 and an AHEAD-style adaptive gain then amplifies this layer specifically in darker image regions:}}
  {Use 6 of this source.}
  {``[Section 2.4 'Adaptive Feature Enhancement', pp. 4-5 (preprint, DOI 10.21203/rs.3.rs-8849147/v1):] ``The algorithm acts as a localized Automatic Gain Control (AGC), inspired by photographic tone reproduction [13], to match the computational edge response with perceptual significance. The adaptive scalar S(x, y) is modeled as a modified inverse-linear function of the local pixel luminance L(x, y) ... Lower luminance areas have higher S values (see Fig. 3), therefore amplifying subtle structural details in shadowed areas. While, higher luminance areas have lower S values, which reduces the effect of applied edge detection ...''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/AHEAD\_Adaptive\_Hierarchical\_Edge\_Detection\_for\_Rea.pdf}. Verified against the primary source. The cited mechanism is exactly as described: a luminance-driven inverse-linear gain that amplifies edge response specifically in darker regions, motivated by the Weber-Fechner Law. One difference the thesis's 'AHEAD-style' wording already signals: AHEAD applies the gain only to its Structure layer (Scharr/Kirsch on image intensity, Section 2.5), never to a world-space normal derivative, and it never applies any operator to the normal map, which appears only as a G-buffer illustration in Fig. 2a.}

\citentry
  {winnemoller2006}
  {Ch.5 Implementation $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Implementation $\rightarrow$ V5 — Hierarchical Edge Detection}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The composited PBR output also contains baked lighting gradients that produce false Roberts Cross responses on this platform. \textbf{The colour layer accordingly replaces Roberts Cross with XDoG: the difference between two Gaussian-blurred luminance samples at different radii, passed through a tanh soft threshold:}}
  {Use 3 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{$\tau$ and $\phi_e$, the two parameters the thesis shader uses} $\leftarrow$ [\S3.2 Edge detection:] ``\textbf{The parameter $\tau$ in Equation 4 controls the amount of center-surround difference required for cell activation, and $\phi_e$ controls the sharpness of the activation falloff.}'' \quad \textbf{(2)} \emph{the tanh soft threshold} $\leftarrow$ [Eq.~4:] $D(\hat{x}, \sigma_e, \tau, \phi_e) = 1$ if $(S_{\sigma_e} - \tau \cdot S_{\sigma_r}) > 0$, and \textbf{$1 + \tanh(\phi_e \cdot (S_{\sigma_e} - \tau \cdot S_{\sigma_r}))$} otherwise. \quad \textbf{(3)} \emph{the radius ratio the shader calls \texttt{\_XDoGK}} $\leftarrow$ [same section:] ``$S_{\sigma_r} \equiv S(\hat{x}, 1.6 \cdot \sigma_e)$\ldots \textbf{The factor of 1.6} relates the typical receptive field of a cell to its surroundings [Marr and Hildreth 1980].''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/videoabstraction.pdf}, \S3.2 and Eq.~4. \textbf{New key added 2026-08-25. Nidanur identified this misattribution herself} while checking \texttt{winnemoller2012}, and it became the worked example behind the origin rule now recorded as CLAUDE.md \S5.3a. \textbf{What was wrong:} the 2012 paper was carrying the operator as though it were its own. It is not. The 2012 compendium credits it inline to its own reference [7], ``Winnemöller, H., Olsen, S.C., Gooch, B.. Real-time video abstraction. ACM Trans Graph 2006;25(3):1221--1226'', and the primary was already held in the Articles folder under a name that did not announce it. \textbf{The decisive evidence is the thesis's own shader.} \texttt{05\_implementation} declares \texttt{\_XDoGTau} and \texttt{\_XDoGPhi}, which are $\tau$ and $\phi_e$ from Equation~4 above. The 2012 paper's own reformulation \textbf{replaces $\tau$ with $p$}, so the parameterisation actually implemented is the 2006 one and not the 2012 one. \textbf{Both keys are cited, deliberately.} \texttt{winnemoller2012} keeps the name \emph{XDoG} and the compendium framing, which are genuinely its contributions. \texttt{winnemoller2006} carries the operator, the tau-weighted difference and the tanh ramp. \textbf{Bibliographic note:} the held PDF is an author version with no DOI or page numbers printed on it. Authors, title and affiliation were read from it directly. Venue, volume, issue, pages 1221--1226 and DOI 10.1145/1141911.1142018 were cross-checked against CrossRef and against the 2012 paper's reference list, which agree exactly. \textbf{Incidental confirmation for Ch.~2:} the 1.6 radius ratio in item (3) is credited by Winnemöller et al.\ to Marr and Hildreth, and it is the same 1.6 that Marr and Hildreth's Appendix~B derives as the optimal ratio for approximating the Laplacian by a difference of Gaussians. The two sections agree.}

\citentry
  {winnemoller2012 --- Winnem\"{o}ller et al. (2012)}
  {Ch.5 Implementation $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Implementation $\rightarrow$ V5 — Hierarchical Edge Detection}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The composited PBR output also contains baked lighting gradients that produce false Roberts Cross responses on this platform. \textbf{The colour layer accordingly replaces Roberts Cross with XDoG: the difference between two Gaussian-blurred luminance samples at different radii, passed through a tanh soft threshold:}}

[§ on parameters:] ``Larger or smaller $\varphi$ control the sharpness of the black/white transitions in the image, while $\varepsilon$ controls the level above which the adjusted luminance values will become white.'' [On $\tau$:] ``replacing $\tau$ with $p$ makes it possible to control the strength of the edge sharpening effect without influencing any other aspects of the filter.''''}

  {Ch.5 Implementation $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Implementation $\rightarrow$ V6 — Kuwahara Painterly Filter}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} \textbf{All three platforms use the anisotropic Kuwahara filter of Kyprianidis et al..} \emph{[This filter relies on a structure tensor to estimate the local feature orientation and anisotropy. The sampling neighbourhood then becomes an ellipse aligned with that orientation rather than a fixed square. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 7 of this source.}
  {``[Title and Abstract (PDF p. 1):] ``Image and Video Abstraction by Anisotropic Kuwahara Filtering ... We present a non-photorealistic rendering technique to transform color images and videos into painterly abstractions. It is based on a generalization of the Kuwahara filter that is adapted to the local shape of features, derived from the smoothed structure tensor.''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_kuwahara.pdf}. Verified against the primary source. The paper supports the attribution only, i.e. that the anisotropic Kuwahara filter is the technique of Kyprianidis et al. The remainder of the sentence ('All three platforms use...') describes the thesis's own implementation and cannot be verified against any source; note that CLAUDE.md 5.1 rule 5 discourages attaching a citation to a sentence stating what was implemented in this work.}

\citentry
  {kyprianidis2009 --- Kyprianidis et al. (2009)}
  {Ch.5 Implementation $\rightarrow$ NPR Technique Implementation $\rightarrow$ V6 — Kuwahara Painterly Filter}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Mixamo and Avaturn scan every pixel within the kernel radius and accumulate it into whichever sector it falls into, and the sample count grows with the radius. The Meta SDK instead takes a fixed three samples per sector at set radii, plus one sample shared across all sectors, for a constant total regardless of radius. Each sector accumulates a mean colour and a variance. These are then blended by an inverse-variance weight, so that the smoothest sector dominates the result. \textbf{That approach follows Kyprianidis et al., who weight each sector inversely to its standard deviation.} \emph{[The Meta SDK applies that form directly. Mixamo and Avaturn instead raise the variance to half the exponent, which is algebraically identical and avoids a square root on every sector. The accumulation that follows the weight is shared across all three platforms: --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 8 of this source.}
  {``[Section 3 'Method' (PDF p. 4) and Section 3.2 'Anisotropic Kuwahara Filtering' (PDF p. 5):] ``The filter response is defined as a weighed sum of the local averages, where more weight is given to those averages with low standard deviation. ... [Section 3.2] ... Our definition of the weighting factors ... ensures that more weight is given to sectors with low standard deviation, i.e. those that are more homogeneous. This is similar to the approach of [PPC07], where ... is used for the weights, but avoids the indetermination, when some of the si are zero.''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/03\_npr\_rendering/anisotropic\_kuwahara.pdf}. \textbf{Resolved 2026-08-22:} the sentence was split: the citation now covers only the inverse-variance weighting principle, and the per-platform difference is stated as this project's own implementation choice. The underlying principle the thesis attributes to the source is genuinely there: weight each sector inversely to its standard deviation. However, the paper defines exactly one weighting formula for its own filter (alpha\_i = 1/(1 + ||s\_i||\textasciicircum{}q), with q = 8 in all examples) and contrasts it only with Papari et al.'s ||s\_i||\textasciicircum{}-q; it does not present a family of interchangeable weighting variants. The idea that different implementations follow 'a different variant' of the formula is the thesis's own implementation choice, not something the paper offers or licenses. Ellipses in the quote replace math glyphs that pdftotext garbles.}

\citentry
  {zell2015 --- Zell et al. (2015)}
  {Ch.6 Evaluation $\rightarrow$ Discussion $\rightarrow$ What the Stylisation Left Unchanged}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} The composite stylises the avatar's material while leaving its shape untouched and this split may be why eeriness did not fall. \textbf{Material governs eeriness in the account of Zell et al. and this alone predicts a reduction for C3.}}
  {Use 6 of this source.}
  {\textbf{[Results, appeal:]} ``We found a \textbf{main effect of material on the ratings of appeal}\dots\ \textbf{but no main effect of shape was found}. An interaction between shape and material ($F(4,84)=13.97$, $p<0.0001$) shows that a realistic material on a cartoon shape yields the least appealing combination\dots\ and \textbf{the cartoon material on the realistic shape is similarly unappealing} ($p<0.02$).'' \quad \textbf{[Results, eeriness:]} the scale is ``Extremely eerie---Extremely re-assuring''; ``we found a \textbf{main effect of material on the ratings of reassurance}\dots\ \textbf{but no main effect was found for shape}.'' \quad \textbf{[Realism:]} ``\textbf{shape is the dominant factor when rating realism} and expression intensity, while \textbf{material is the key component for appeal}.'' \quad \textbf{[Conclusion, the mismatch framing in the authors' own words:]} ``\textbf{Matching levels of stylization of geometry and material cause the highest ratings of appeal, while strong mismatches (e.g., very realistic material on a stylized shape) result in unappealing and eerie characters}.''}
  {Read from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/To Stylize or not to Stylize?\_Zell.pdf} (13 pages). \textbf{Verified 2026-08-22.} All three thesis claims hold. The mismatch framing is the authors' own conclusion, not an inference. Two further findings in the same passage are not yet used in the thesis and would strengthen Evaluation \S6.3: ``the effect of material stylizations grows when shape realism is increased\dots\ mismatches \dots\ are less prominent on abstract characters'' (C3 has a photogrammetric shape, so material mismatch bites hardest there), and ``subtle stylization of a realistic material (edge-preserving blur on the albedo texture) increases appeal without sacrificing realism'' (essentially the head-texture preprocessing step).}

\citentry
  {zell2015 --- Zell et al. (2015)}
  {Ch.6 Evaluation $\rightarrow$ Discussion $\rightarrow$ What the Stylisation Left Unchanged}
  {\textbf{[Block covered by this citation]} Their mismatch result points the other way. C3 sets a stylised material on a photogrammetrically reconstructed shape. \textbf{Zell et al. also reported that the influence of material stylisation on perceived realism grows as shape realism rises, and this places C3 at the point where a mismatch would be most visible.} \emph{[The abstraction may consequently have removed eeriness from the material only to reintroduce it through the gap between the two. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 7 of this source.}
  {\textbf{(1)} \emph{``the influence of material stylisation on perceived realism grows as shape realism rises''} $\leftarrow$ [\S5.2:] ``This is in line with the results from Section 5.1, and \textbf{confirms that as the shape becomes more realistic, the material stylization becomes more dominant for perceived realism}.''}
  {Read directly from \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/Zell2015\_StyleShape.pdf}, \S5.2. \textbf{Scope drift corrected 2026-08-25.} The thesis sentence dropped the outcome variable, leaving ``the influence of material stylisation grows as shape realism rises'' with no dependent measure attached. The source scopes that growth to \textbf{perceived realism}. The omission is not harmless here, because the surrounding paragraph argues about \textbf{eeriness} throughout: it opens on ``this split may be why eeriness did not fall'' and closes on ``removed eeriness from the material only to reintroduce it. An unqualified sentence lets a realism result read as an eeriness result inside an eeriness argument, and Zell et al.\ measure the two separately and reach different conclusions on each. The argument itself is unaffected, since the point being made is that C3 sits where material stylisation dominates. \textbf{Style fix in the same pass:} the trailing ``which'' clause became ``and this'' per the split rule.}

\citentry

The stylisation on this reading introduced a new source of uncanniness even as it removed another. --- follows the citation, uncited.]}}
  {Use 2 of this source.}
  {``[Section 1 (Introduction), printed p. 02 (PDF p. 2):] ``Understanding UVE remains challenging due to the existence of multiple competing hypotheses that try to explain our perception of anthropomorphism in ECAs. These range from the morbidity and movement hypotheses to the category ambiguity (Cheetham and Jancke, 2013; Katsyri et al., 2015; Pollick, 2010). Among these, the perceptual mismatch hypothesis has received strong empirical support.''''}
  {File: \texttt{\footnotesize Articles/01\_uncanny\_valley/The\_uncanny\_valley\_effect\_in\_embodied\_conversation.pdf}. \textbf{Resolved 2026-08-22:} reworded to ``has received strong empirical support'', matching the source exactly. The authors do single the perceptual mismatch hypothesis out from the competing hypotheses, but they write that it 'has received strong empirical support', not the strongest; no ranking of the hypotheses by weight of evidence appears anywhere in the paper. The thesis converts a positive into a superlative, so 'strong empirical support among competing explanations' would match the source, while 'the strongest' overstates it. Searched the full text for 'mismatch', 'strongest', 'most support' and 'competing hypothes'; the only other substantive mentions are the Discussion's own framework (mismatch detection between perceived anthropomorphism and user expectations, p. 15) and the Katsyri et al. (2015) reference entry.}

  {\textbf{CONFIRMED (corrected 2026-08-14).} The earlier wording claimed a ``weaker and less consistent effect on social presence,'' which contradicted the source: social presence was significantly higher for the realistic character ($p = 0.025$, $d = 0.56$). That no-effect result belongs to \texttt{zibrek2019tap}, against which this paper explicitly contrasts itself, and the two Zibrek findings had been conflated. The thesis sentence now states both significant self-report increases and the unaffected behavioural proximity measure, matching the source.}
